### Scaling up Multi-Turn Off-Policy RL and Multi-Agent Tree Search for LLM Step-Provers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06493v1)
- **Authors**: Ran Xin, Zeyu Zheng, Yanchen Nie, Kun Yuan, Xia Xiao
- **Abstract**: The integration of Large Language Models (LLMs) into automated theorem proving has shown immense promise, yet is fundamentally constrained by challenges in scaling up both training-time reinforcement learning (RL) and inference-time compute. This paper introduces \texttt{BFS-Prover-V2}, a system designed to address this dual scaling problem. We present two primary innovations. The first is a novel multi-turn off-policy RL framework for continually improving the performance of LLM step-prover at training time. This framework, inspired by the principles of AlphaZero, utilizes a multi-stage expert iteration pipeline featuring adaptive tactic-level data filtering and periodic retraining to surmount the performance plateaus that typically curtail long-term RL in LLM-based agents. The second innovation is a planner-enhanced multi-agent search architecture that scales reasoning capabilities at inference time. This architecture employs a general reasoning model as a high-level planner to iteratively decompose complex theorems into a sequence of simpler subgoals. This hierarchical approach substantially reduces the search space, enabling a team of parallel prover agents to collaborate efficiently by leveraging a shared proof cache. We demonstrate that this dual approach to scaling yields state-of-the-art results on established formal mathematics benchmarks. \texttt{BFS-Prover-V2} achieves 95.08\% and 41.4\% on the MiniF2F and ProofNet test sets respectively. While demonstrated in the domain of formal mathematics, the RL and inference techniques presented in this work are of broader interest and may be applied to other domains requiring long-horizon multi-turn reasoning and complex search.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BFS-Prover-V2, a system designed to improve the performance of LLM-based automated theorem provers. It addresses the challenge of scaling up both training-time reinforcement learning (RL) and inference-time compute in the context of formal mathematics. The system incorporates two primary innovations:

1.  A novel multi-turn off-policy RL framework is developed for training. This framework uses expert iteration with adaptive tactic-level data filtering and periodic retraining to avoid performance plateaus.
2.  A planner-enhanced multi-agent tree search architecture for inference is implemented, where a general reasoning model decomposes complex theorems into simpler subgoals, which are then tackled by parallel prover agents leveraging a shared proof cache.

The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art results on the MiniF2F and ProofNet benchmarks, showcasing the effectiveness of the dual scaling approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a significant step forward in scaling LLM-based theorem provers. The combination of off-policy RL with adaptive filtering and periodic retraining addresses a crucial bottleneck in training LLM-based agents. The planner-enhanced multi-agent architecture provides an innovative solution for scaling reasoning capabilities at inference time. While expert iteration and multi-agent search are not entirely new concepts, their adaptation and specific implementation in this context, especially with the data filtering and periodic retraining techniques, represent genuine advancements. The hierarchical reasoning approach during inference is also a valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:** The results on MiniF2F and ProofNet demonstrate that BFS-Prover-V2 achieves state-of-the-art performance among LLM step-provers. The success on ProofNet, in particular, highlights the system's ability to generalize to more complex problems. These improvements significantly expand the scope of problems that can be tackled by LLM-based automated reasoning, potentially impacting the field of formal verification and neuro-symbolic AI.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a clear and well-structured description of the system architecture and algorithms.
    *   The experimental results are compelling and demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed techniques.
    *   The paper thoroughly addresses the problem of scaling, offering solutions for both training and inference challenges.
    *   The approach is not limited to formal mathematics and can be potentially transferred to other domains with complex search and reasoning needs.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper mentions the use of 3 million automatically formalized problems for training, there could be more detailed analysis regarding data quality and selection bias.
    *   The paper does not fully explore the limitations of the planner-prover paradigm or the challenges in generalizing to theorems that require more complex planning strategies or intermediate steps.
    *   The reliance on commercially available models (Qwen and Gemini) creates some reproducibility concerns if the models and APIs change.
    *   A more in-depth comparison with *whole proof generation models* would give context to the claimed *comparable* nature. While the method focuses on interactive theorem proving, clarifying how its benefits would impact those in the generation of full proofs would strengthen the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The work has the potential to significantly influence future research in automated theorem proving, neuro-symbolic AI, and RL. The techniques for overcoming performance plateaus in RL and scaling inference-time reasoning are applicable to a broader range of domains beyond formal mathematics. The results may also prompt further investigation into hierarchical reasoning architectures and the use of LLMs as planners in complex problem-solving tasks.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper demonstrates clear advancements in both training and inference techniques for LLM-based automated theorem provers. The experimental results provide strong empirical support for the effectiveness of the proposed techniques, establishing BFS-Prover-V2 as a leading system in the field. While there are some limitations regarding data analysis and generalization, the overall impact of the paper is significant, and it offers a valuable contribution to the ongoing research in AI and formal reasoning.

### TIDE: Achieving Balanced Subject-Driven Image Generation via Target-Instructed Diffusion Enhancement
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06499v1)
- **Authors**: Jibai Lin, Bo Ma, Yating Yang, Rong Ma, Turghun Osman, Ahtamjan Ahmat, Rui Dong, Lei Wang, Xi Zhou
- **Abstract**: Subject-driven image generation (SDIG) aims to manipulate specific subjects within images while adhering to textual instructions, a task crucial for advancing text-to-image diffusion models. SDIG requires reconciling the tension between maintaining subject identity and complying with dynamic edit instructions, a challenge inadequately addressed by existing methods. In this paper, we introduce the Target-Instructed Diffusion Enhancing (TIDE) framework, which resolves this tension through target supervision and preference learning without test-time fine-tuning. TIDE pioneers target-supervised triplet alignment, modelling subject adaptation dynamics using a (reference image, instruction, target images) triplet. This approach leverages the Direct Subject Diffusion (DSD) objective, training the model with paired "winning" (balanced preservation-compliance) and "losing" (distorted) targets, systematically generated and evaluated via quantitative metrics. This enables implicit reward modelling for optimal preservation-compliance balance. Experimental results on standard benchmarks demonstrate TIDE's superior performance in generating subject-faithful outputs while maintaining instruction compliance, outperforming baseline methods across multiple quantitative metrics. TIDE's versatility is further evidenced by its successful application to diverse tasks, including structural-conditioned generation, image-to-image generation, and text-image interpolation. Our code is available at https://github.com/KomJay520/TIDE.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TIDE: Achieving Balanced Subject-Driven Image Generation via Target-Instructed Diffusion Enhancement":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TIDE, a novel framework for subject-driven image generation (SDIG) designed to balance the often-competing goals of preserving subject identity and adhering to textual instructions. TIDE leverages target supervision and preference learning without requiring test-time fine-tuning. The core innovations are: 1) a target-supervised triplet alignment (reference image, instruction, target image), and 2) a Direct Subject Diffusion (DSD) objective that trains the model using paired "winning" and "losing" target images generated and evaluated through quantitative metrics. The paper demonstrates TIDE's superior performance compared to existing methods on standard benchmarks, highlighting its ability to generate subject-faithful outputs while maintaining instruction compliance. The framework's versatility is further showcased through its application to tasks like structure-conditioned generation, image-to-image generation, and text-image interpolation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   **Strengths:** The key novelty lies in the introduction of explicit triplet supervision. Prior SDIG methods relied primarily on self-supervised reconstruction objectives, using reference images as pseudo-targets. The structured guidance provided by the reference-instruction-target triplet offers a significant advantage in disentangling preservation and modification. The DSD objective, adapting pairwise preference learning to SDIG by integrating subject-specific features, is also innovative. By using  "winning" and "losing" target pairs and preference-aware gradients, the model learns which features must be modified to achieve instruction compliance while preserving subject fidelity.
    *   **Weaknesses:** Some components of the architecture, such as the use of CLIP encoders and cross-attention mechanisms, are inspired by existing techniques. The Image Projection Module (IPM) and Image Cross-Attention Module (ICAM), while effective, are relatively straightforward adaptations of existing cross-attention mechanisms. However, the primary contribution is not necessarily the architecture itself but the novel training procedure and objectives.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Strengths:** SDIG is a rapidly growing field, and the challenges addressed by TIDE (balancing subject preservation and instruction compliance) are fundamental. Achieving a good balance is important to produce realistic and usable results in different areas like personalised advertising. TIDE's capability to avoid test-time fine-tuning makes it more efficient and scalable than methods requiring per-subject optimization. Also, The paper demonstrates that the architecture generalizes to other tasks like structural conditional generation, further expanding the applications of the work.
    *   **Weaknesses:** While the paper demonstrates superior performance on standard benchmarks, the improvement in DINO score on DreamBench is not significant. Moreover, the use of automated evaluation can be questioned because there is not enough comparison with real, human feedback. Also, it would have been nice to see the architecture working with bigger diffusion models (the experiments were done with SD 1.5).

*   **Impact:**

    *   **Strengths:** TIDE's approach to combining existing text-to-image diffusion models with subject-driven image generation and DPO is a solid improvement in generating more accurate images. Also, the open source code will likely encourage further research and development in the field.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The reliance on the CLIP encoders does introduce well-documented biases with these models. These biases could propagate to generations from the system, which is a significant limitation.

**Justification for Score:**

TIDE represents a significant advancement in SDIG by introducing a more principled and effective training approach. The novelty of target supervision and the DSD objective, combined with the practical advantages of fine-tuning-free operation, warrants a positive evaluation. While the architectural components are not entirely revolutionary, their integration with the novel training scheme and the resulting performance improvements justify a high score. It addresses a core challenge in the field and makes valuable contributions by combining existing diffusion models with a preference optimization and subject-driven image generation approach. The downsides include not generating huge improvements with all metrics, relying on CLIP encoders, and using SD 1.5.

### WebExplorer: Explore and Evolve for Training Long-Horizon Web Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06501v1)
- **Authors**: Junteng Liu, Yunji Li, Chi Zhang, Jingyang Li, Aili Chen, Ke Ji, Weiyu Cheng, Zijia Wu, Chengyu Du, Qidi Xu, Jiayuan Song, Zhengmao Zhu, Wenhu Chen, Pengyu Zhao, Junxian He
- **Abstract**: The paradigm of Large Language Models (LLMs) has increasingly shifted toward agentic applications, where web browsing capabilities are fundamental for retrieving information from diverse online sources. However, existing open-source web agents either demonstrate limited information-seeking abilities on complex tasks or lack transparent implementations. In this work, we identify that the key challenge lies in the scarcity of challenging data for information seeking. To address this limitation, we introduce WebExplorer: a systematic data generation approach using model-based exploration and iterative, long-to-short query evolution. This method creates challenging query-answer pairs that require multi-step reasoning and complex web navigation. By leveraging our curated high-quality dataset, we successfully develop advanced web agent WebExplorer-8B through supervised fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning. Our model supports 128K context length and up to 100 tool calling turns, enabling long-horizon problem solving. Across diverse information-seeking benchmarks, WebExplorer-8B achieves the state-of-the-art performance at its scale. Notably, as an 8B-sized model, WebExplorer-8B is able to effectively search over an average of 16 turns after RL training, achieving higher accuracy than WebSailor-72B on BrowseComp-en/zh and attaining the best performance among models up to 100B parameters on WebWalkerQA and FRAMES. Beyond these information-seeking tasks, our model also achieves strong generalization on the HLE benchmark even though it is only trained on knowledge-intensive QA data. These results highlight our approach as a practical path toward long-horizon web agents.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces WebEXPLORER, a novel data generation approach for training web-browsing agents. It addresses the scarcity of challenging, high-quality training data, identified as a key bottleneck in developing capable web agents. WebEXPLORER employs two main techniques: 1) Model-based exploration, which uses LLMs to simulate web browsing and build an information space from a seed entity; and 2) Iterative long-to-short query evolution, which refines initial query-answer pairs by systematically removing salient information to increase difficulty.  The authors then use supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning (GRPO) to train WebEXPLORER-8B, an 8B parameter model based on Qwen3-8B. The resulting agent achieves state-of-the-art performance at its scale on several information-seeking benchmarks, demonstrating its effectiveness in handling complex, long-horizon web navigation tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main contribution lies in the data generation methodology.  The combination of model-based exploration *and* iterative query evolution to create challenging training data is a significant step forward. While model-based data generation and query evolution techniques exist in isolation, the specific way WebEXPLORER combines them, particularly the *long-to-short* query evolution process, is novel. The idea of deliberately obscuring information to create harder tasks instead of injecting new complexity directly is a unique element. The benchmarks used are established, so the evaluation setup is not a novelty in itself, but using the data generated for training and showing state-of-the-art results in several benchmarks reinforces the novelty of data generation.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of the work stems from the practical problem it addresses: the need for better training data for web agents.  By developing a system that can autonomously generate this data, WebEXPLORER has the potential to greatly accelerate progress in the field. The impressive performance of WebEXPLORER-8B, achieving state-of-the-art results for an 8B model and outperforming much larger models on several benchmarks, directly validates this potential. The generalization to the HLE benchmark, despite not being specifically trained for it, further strengthens the argument that the data generation process creates robust and generalizable agents. The scaling experiments, taking it all the way to 128K context length, highlights that the approach is also practical.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem statement:** The paper clearly identifies a critical bottleneck in web agent development.
    *   **Novel methodology:** The data generation approach is well-motivated and innovative.
    *   **Strong experimental results:**  The results on a variety of benchmarks convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of WebEXPLORER.
    *   **Practical application:**  The resulting agent, WebEXPLORER-8B, is a practical tool that can be used for real-world web navigation tasks.
    *   **Detailed description:** The paper provides sufficient detail for others to reproduce and build upon the work.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited ablation studies:** While the paper shows strong results, it would be valuable to see ablation studies that isolate the contributions of the individual components of WebEXPLORER. For example, comparing performance with and without the iterative query evolution process.
    *   **Reliance on closed-source LLMs:** The data generation pipeline relies on powerful closed-source models (OpenAI and Claude). This could limit the reproducibility and accessibility of the work for some researchers. This point is not that critical considering that the training happens after the dataset generation and the trained model itself (Qwen3-8B) is opensource.
    *   **Further Dataset analysis**: A more in-depth analysis of the generated dataset could offer additional insights. For example, analyzing the types of queries that are particularly challenging for existing models, or investigating the characteristics of the information spaces that are explored by the model.

*   **Potential Influence:** The work has the potential to influence future research in web agent development by providing a new approach to data generation and by demonstrating the effectiveness of long-to-short evolution. It can also lead to a better understanding of how to create challenging reasoning tasks for web agents. The availability of the generated dataset (presumably, as the paper mentions that the code will be released) would greatly accelerate progress in the field.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

WebEXPLORER represents a solid contribution to the field of web agent development. The combination of exploration with long-to-short query evolution offers a distinct advantage over existing methods for generating challenging training data. The strong performance of WebEXPLORER-8B across several benchmarks demonstrates the practical value of this approach.  While the paper could benefit from further ablation studies and a more in-depth dataset analysis, its novelty, significance, and potential influence warrant a high score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### LAMDAS: LLM as an Implicit Classifier for Domain-specific Data Selection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06524v1)
- **Authors**: Jian Wu, Hang Yu, Bingchang Liu, Wenjie Yang, Peng Di, Jianguo Li, Yue Zhang
- **Abstract**: Adapting large language models (LLMs) to specific domains often faces a critical bottleneck: the scarcity of high-quality, human-curated data. While large volumes of unchecked data are readily available, indiscriminately using them for fine-tuning risks introducing noise and degrading performance. Strategic data selection is thus crucial, requiring a method that is both accurate and efficient. Existing approaches, categorized as similarity-based and direct optimization methods, struggle to simultaneously achieve these goals. In this paper, we introduce LAMDAS (LLM As an iMplicit classifier for domain-specific DAta Selection), a novel approach that leverages the pre-trained LLM itself as an implicit classifier, thereby bypassing explicit feature engineering and computationally intensive optimization process. LAMDAS reframes data selection as a one-class classification problem, identifying candidate data that "belongs" to the target domain defined by a small reference dataset. Extensive experimental results demonstrate that LAMDAS not only exceeds the performance of full-data training using a fraction of the data but also outperforms nine state-of-the-art (SOTA) baselines under various scenarios. Furthermore, LAMDAS achieves the most compelling balance between performance gains and computational efficiency compared to all evaluated baselines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LAMDAS: LLM as an Implicit Classifier for Domain-specific Data Selection":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LAMDAS, a novel approach for selecting domain-specific data to improve the performance of large language models (LLMs) when adapting them to specialized tasks via continual pre-training (CPT) or supervised fine-tuning (SFT). LAMDAS leverages the pre-trained LLM itself as an *implicit classifier* for data selection.  It reframes data selection as a one-class classification (OCC) problem, where a small reference dataset defines the target domain.  The approach learns a "domain prefix" by tuning a small prefix on the reference dataset.  Candidate data examples are scored based on the likelihood ratio of the LLM generating them *with* and *without* the domain prefix;  examples with a significantly higher likelihood when conditioned on the prefix are prioritized. Extensive experiments on coding and mathematical reasoning demonstrate that LAMDAS outperforms several state-of-the-art (SOTA) baselines while also achieving a more favorable trade-off between performance gains and computational efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using the LLM itself as an *implicit* one-class classifier is innovative.  Traditional data selection methods rely on explicit feature engineering or computationally expensive optimization. LAMDAS cleverly bypasses both by leveraging the LLM's inherent knowledge.  Reframing the problem as OCC and using a *prefix* learned on the reference set is a strong contribution. The connection to classifier-free guidance from diffusion models adds further depth. This shows a fresh perspective on a data selection paradigm.

*   **Significance:** Data selection is a critical bottleneck in LLM adaptation, particularly with the increasing availability of large, unchecked datasets. LAMDAS tackles this problem directly by being both *accurate* (improving performance) and *efficient* (reducing computational cost). The ability to outperform SOTA baselines with a fraction of the data is a significant achievement. This method could be useful to researchers and practitioners with limited computational resources who want to adapt LLMs effectively. LAMDAS focuses on domain adaptation in CPT and SFT setups which is valuable in real world applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Performance:** The empirical results are compelling, demonstrating substantial improvements over a wide range of baselines and tasks. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the sensitivity of LAMDAS to various parameters.
    *   **Efficiency:** LAMDAS achieves an excellent balance between performance and computational cost. The likelihood ratio scoring and prefix tuning are relatively efficient compared to gradient-based methods or complex optimization techniques.
    *   **Conceptual Clarity:** The paper clearly explains the methodology and its connection to other areas like OCC and classifier-free guidance.
    *   **Well-structured and Thorough:**  The experimental setup is comprehensive, and the results are well-presented.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** While the ablation studies explored the impact of key parameters, a more systematic analysis of hyperparameter tuning might be beneficial. The optimal prefix length and likelihood ratio can be explored in depth.
    *   **Scalability:** The paper indicates that LAMDAS scales well by processing billions of tokens on multiple GPUs. However, a deeper discussion of the limitations and challenges of scaling to extremely large datasets or different LLM architectures would be valuable. Although the authors do mention that the smaller LLMs can be used for classification, the results for data selection on LLMs should have been investigated to get a fair comparison against larger datasets.
    *   **Generalizability:** Even though the experiments cover coding and mathematical reasoning, further validation on a wider range of domains would strengthen the generalizability of the approach.
    *   **Implicit negative samples**: The paper does not show why the data examples that have been filtered out will act as negative examples, because ultimately these examples that have been filtered out are relevant to the original task.
    *   **Limited insights in mathematical reasoning**: Mathematical data selection is relatively less investigated, with the majority of selection methods oriented towards language data tasks. Therefore, it is unclear that selecting these sets on these specific settings is novel.

*   **Potential Influence:** LAMDAS has the potential to significantly influence the field of LLM adaptation by providing a more efficient and effective data selection strategy. It could inspire further research into implicit classification techniques and the use of LLMs for self-supervision in data selection. Also, the data selection code will be available upon publication as stated in the abstract.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:**

LAMDAS represents a significant advance in domain-specific data selection for LLMs. The core idea is novel and well-executed, with strong empirical results demonstrating its effectiveness and efficiency. While the paper has some limitations regarding hyperparameter sensitivity, scalability, and a small set of tasks, the overall contribution is substantial. The method fills a gap in the current landscape of data selection techniques, especially for scenarios with limited computational resources. Therefore, I give it a score of 8, reflecting its significant contribution and potential to influence future research in this area.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### SLiNT: Structure-aware Language Model with Injection and Contrastive Training for Knowledge Graph Completion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06531v1)
- **Authors**: Mengxue Yang, Chun Yang, Jiaqi Zhu, Jiafan Li, Jingqi Zhang, Yuyang Li, Ying Li
- **Abstract**: Link prediction in knowledge graphs requires integrating structural information and semantic context to infer missing entities. While large language models offer strong generative reasoning capabilities, their limited exploitation of structural signals often results in structural sparsity and semantic ambiguity, especially under incomplete or zero-shot settings. To address these challenges, we propose SLiNT (Structure-aware Language model with Injection and coNtrastive Training), a modular framework that injects knowledge-graph-derived structural context into a frozen LLM backbone with lightweight LoRA-based adaptation for robust link prediction. Specifically, Structure-Guided Neighborhood Enhancement (SGNE) retrieves pseudo-neighbors to enrich sparse entities and mitigate missing context; Dynamic Hard Contrastive Learning (DHCL) introduces fine-grained supervision by interpolating hard positives and negatives to resolve entity-level ambiguity; and Gradient-Decoupled Dual Injection (GDDI) performs token-level structure-aware intervention while preserving the core LLM parameters. Experiments on WN18RR and FB15k-237 show that SLiNT achieves superior or competitive performance compared with both embedding-based and generation-based baselines, demonstrating the effectiveness of structure-aware representation learning for scalable knowledge graph completion.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SLINT, a novel framework for knowledge graph completion (KGC) that leverages large language models (LLMs). SLINT addresses two key challenges in KGC: structural sparsity and semantic ambiguity. It achieves this through three main modules: Structure-Guided Neighborhood Enhancement (SGNE) to enrich sparse entities, Dynamic Hard Contrastive Learning (DHCL) to resolve entity-level ambiguity, and Gradient-Decoupled Dual Injection (GDDI) to inject structural context into the LLM.  The framework uses a frozen LLM backbone adapted with lightweight LoRA tuning. Experimental results on WN18RR and FB15k-237 demonstrate that SLINT achieves superior or competitive performance compared to existing embedding-based and generation-based approaches.  Ablation studies further validate the contribution of each module. The authors also show SLINT's robustness under low-resource conditions and demonstrate qualitative improvements through case studies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper introduces a fairly innovative combination of techniques. While individual components like contrastive learning, neighborhood aggregation, and prompt engineering have been explored in the context of KGC and LLMs, the integrated SLINT architecture, specifically designed to address structural sparsity *and* semantic ambiguity with carefully engineered components (SGNE, DHCL, GDDI) provides a novel approach. The GDDI module, in particular, appears to be a significant contribution, allowing for efficient and effective structure injection into a frozen LLM. The dynamic hard contrastive learning mechanism also adds a non-trivial element of novelty.
*   **Significance:**  The significance lies in achieving improved performance and robustness in KGC using LLMs, especially under challenging scenarios like sparse or ambiguous KGs.  The paper addresses a critical limitation of existing LLM-based KGC methods by explicitly incorporating structural information in a well-designed manner. The modular framework allows flexible combination with existing pretrained KG embeddings. The efficiency of the LoRA adaptation is also a crucial aspect making it scalable and practical. Furthermore, it establishes a new approach for incorporating structured data into language models without requiring full fine-tuning, opening doors for further exploration of hybrid models.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong empirical results on benchmark datasets.
    *   Comprehensive ablation studies demonstrating the value of each component.
    *   Addresses a key limitation of LLM-based KGC (structural sparsity and semantic ambiguity).
    *   Efficient design using LoRA tuning and cached precomputation.
    *   Well-written and clearly explained methodology.
    *   Provides useful analysis of robustness and limitation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reliance on pretrained KG embeddings somewhat limits the applicability to scenarios where such embeddings are unavailable or outdated.
    *   While the paper claims efficient adaptation via LoRA, a direct comparison of training and inference cost against a fully fine-tuned LLM (even if impractical) would have strengthened the argument.  (Comparison against DIFT is helpful, but not the same.)
    *   The case study, while illustrative, is limited in scope and doesn't offer a quantitative analysis of the types of errors SLINT corrects or fails to correct.
    *   The limitations section mentions the lack of multimodal capabilities, and while a future research direction, it would provide a more well-rounded assessment of its capabilities if it were more applicable.

*   **Impact:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in KGC and LLMs by providing a promising approach for effectively integrating structural knowledge into LLMs. It opens up avenues for exploring other forms of structured data injection and adaptive tuning strategies. It could also inspire the development of more robust and scalable KGC systems for real-world applications. However, its impact will depend on further adoption and extensions by the research community.

**Justification of Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7.5**.

*   The approach taken and implemented demonstrates a thoughtful understanding of the limitations of using LLMs for KGC, and the authors address those limitations with a novel framework of combined techniques. While the individual techniques are well-known, the integrated approach (SGNE, DHCL, and GDDI) has significant novelty that makes the whole greater than the sum of the parts. The robust experiments with ablation studies make a strong case for the approach. However, some limitations detract from a higher score, particularly the reliance on pre-trained KG embeddings and the fairly limited case study. Additional work is needed to address these issues and further improve the framework's robustness and generality. Nonetheless, it is a solid contribution to the field, and its design is efficient for its intended purposes.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reasoning-enhanced Query Understanding through Decomposition and Interpretation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06544v1)
- **Authors**: Yunfei Zhong, Jun Yang, Yixing Fan, Jiafeng Guo, Lixin Su, Maarten de Rijke, Ruqing Zhang, Dawei Yin, Xueqi Cheng
- **Abstract**: Accurate inference of user intent is crucial for enhancing document retrieval in modern search engines. While large language models (LLMs) have made significant strides in this area, their effectiveness has predominantly been assessed with short, keyword-based queries. As AI-driven search evolves, long-form queries with intricate intents are becoming more prevalent, yet they remain underexplored in the context of LLM-based query understanding (QU). To bridge this gap, we introduce ReDI: a Reasoning-enhanced approach for query understanding through Decomposition and Interpretation. ReDI leverages the reasoning and comprehension capabilities of LLMs in a three-stage pipeline: (i) it breaks down complex queries into targeted sub-queries to accurately capture user intent; (ii) it enriches each sub-query with detailed semantic interpretations to improve the query-document matching; and (iii) it independently retrieves documents for each sub-query and employs a fusion strategy to aggregate the results for the final ranking. We compiled a large-scale dataset of real-world complex queries from a major search engine and distilled the query understanding capabilities of teacher models into smaller models for practical application. Experiments on BRIGHT and BEIR demonstrate that ReDI consistently surpasses strong baselines in both sparse and dense retrieval paradigms, affirming its effectiveness.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Reasoning-enhanced Query Understanding through Decomposition and Interpretation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ReDI, a novel framework for query understanding (QU) designed to handle complex, intent-driven queries in modern search engines.  ReDI leverages large language models (LLMs) in a three-stage process: (1) decomposing complex queries into targeted sub-queries; (2) enriching each sub-query with detailed semantic interpretations for better query-document matching; and (3) independently retrieving documents for each sub-query and employing a fusion strategy to aggregate the results. The authors create a new large-scale dataset (COIN) of real-world complex queries and distill the knowledge from a large teacher LLM (DeepSeek-R1) into a smaller, production-ready student model. Experiments on BRIGHT and BEIR benchmarks demonstrate that ReDI consistently outperforms strong baselines in both sparse and dense retrieval paradigms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of query decomposition is not entirely new. However, the paper makes a significant contribution by integrating it with sub-query interpretation and specifically tailoring this approach for *complex* queries that necessitate reasoning.  The three-stage pipeline is well-defined, and the use of LLMs for both decomposition *and* interpretation represents a novel synthesis. The data distillation aspect to make the approach practical is also a positive contribution. The dataset is arguably a significant contribution to spur research on this topic of complex queries, which has been largely ignored.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a growing need in AI-driven search: effectively handling complex, intent-driven queries beyond simple keyword searches. By outperforming existing methods on challenging benchmarks like BRIGHT and BEIR, the paper shows that ReDI has the potential to improve search accuracy and user satisfaction in modern search engines. The focus on transferring the capabilities of larger LLMs to smaller models is vital for practical deployment and cost-effectiveness. The impact on the field can be substantial, potentially leading to a shift in focus towards handling complex queries in a more sophisticated and LLM-integrated manner. The release of COIN is an important contribution that lowers the barriers for others to develop and evaluate QU methods for this type of complex search task.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined and modular framework (ReDI).
    *   Creation of a large-scale, real-world complex query dataset (COIN).
    *   Thorough experimental evaluation on multiple benchmarks (BRIGHT, BEIR).
    *   Demonstrated effectiveness in both sparse and dense retrieval settings.
    *   Emphasis on practical deployment through knowledge distillation.
    *   Ablation studies and analyses that provide insights into the method's behavior.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the paper claims the distilled student model matches or even surpasses the teacher in generating high-quality intent-aware queries, it does not extensively show comparative query outputs or qualitative analysis. Providing more concrete examples of the generated interpretations and sub-queries would further strengthen the claims.
    *   The choice of the Qwen3-8B model is not particularly justified, other than the cost advantages. It would be stronger to show a systematic analysis to show the tradeoffs among various open source models.
    *   Although the prompts designed for different retrieval methods are mentioned, they aren't described thoroughly. More details on the prompt engineering aspect are important for reproducibility.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:**

    This paper represents a solid and useful contribution to the field of query understanding. The focus on complex queries, the integration of decomposition and interpretation using LLMs, and the data distillation for practical application are valuable elements. The strong empirical results and the release of the COIN dataset further bolster the paper's impact. However, some weaknesses exist in the evaluation and in justifying certain implementation choices. Given the significance of the research problem and the contributions made, while acknowledging the noted weaknesses, the paper merits a favorable score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mind Your Server: A Systematic Study of Parasitic Toolchain Attacks on the MCP Ecosystem
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06572v1)
- **Authors**: Shuli Zhao, Qinsheng Hou, Zihan Zhan, Yanhao Wang, Yuchong Xie, Yu Guo, Libo Chen, Shenghong Li, Zhi Xue
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated with external systems through the Model Context Protocol (MCP), which standardizes tool invocation and has rapidly become a backbone for LLM-powered applications. While this paradigm enhances functionality, it also introduces a fundamental security shift: LLMs transition from passive information processors to autonomous orchestrators of task-oriented toolchains, expanding the attack surface, elevating adversarial goals from manipulating single outputs to hijacking entire execution flows. In this paper, we reveal a new class of attacks, Parasitic Toolchain Attacks, instantiated as MCP Unintended Privacy Disclosure (MCP-UPD). These attacks require no direct victim interaction; instead, adversaries embed malicious instructions into external data sources that LLMs access during legitimate tasks. The malicious logic infiltrates the toolchain and unfolds in three phases: Parasitic Ingestion, Privacy Collection, and Privacy Disclosure, culminating in stealthy exfiltration of private data. Our root cause analysis reveals that MCP lacks both context-tool isolation and least-privilege enforcement, enabling adversarial instructions to propagate unchecked into sensitive tool invocations. To assess the severity, we design MCP-SEC and conduct the first large-scale security census of the MCP ecosystem, analyzing 12,230 tools across 1,360 servers. Our findings show that the MCP ecosystem is rife with exploitable gadgets and diverse attack methods, underscoring systemic risks in MCP platforms and the urgent need for defense mechanisms in LLM-integrated environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Mind Your Server: A Systematic Study of Parasitic Toolchain Attacks on the MCP Ecosystem":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new class of attacks called "Parasitic Toolchain Attacks" on the Model Context Protocol (MCP) ecosystem, specifically focusing on MCP Unintended Privacy Disclosure (MCP-UPD). The attacks involve embedding malicious instructions into external data sources that LLMs access during legitimate tasks, leading to stealthy exfiltration of private data.  The paper outlines a three-phase process: Parasitic Ingestion, Privacy Collection, and Privacy Disclosure.  The authors identify the lack of context-tool isolation and least-privilege enforcement in MCP as the root causes of this vulnerability. They design a framework, MCP-SEC, and conduct a large-scale security census analyzing thousands of tools and servers on public MCP platforms, demonstrating the prevalence and diversity of exploitable gadgets and attack methods. Finally, the paper proposes defense mechanisms and discusses other potential parasitic toolchain attacks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper presents a novel and timely threat model. While prompt injection and LLM security issues have been widely studied, the specific focus on the MCP ecosystem and the concept of "parasitic toolchains" offer a fresh perspective on the attack surface. The shift from LLMs as information processors to autonomous orchestrators is a crucial insight. The idea of passively injecting malicious instructions through external data sources without direct victim interaction is innovative and highlights a previously underexplored vulnerability. The formalization of the MCP-UPD attack class is a significant contribution.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its practical implications for the burgeoning LLM-integrated application landscape. MCP is becoming a crucial integration protocol, and this study highlights the potential for widespread vulnerabilities. The large-scale analysis of real-world MCP deployments (over 12,000 tools and 1300+ servers) provides empirical evidence supporting the severity of the threat. The identification of vulnerable tools (nearly half of all MCP tools) and servers (nearly 80% of servers) provides a strong argument for the urgent need for defense mechanisms. The analysis of the different kinds of MCP servers contributes to the understanding of the kinds of deployments that are more likely to be at risk. Also the research questions that the paper sets to answer helps to understand how the findings would contribute to the ecosystem.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined Threat Model:** The paper clearly articulates the threat model, including attacker goals, capabilities, and system assumptions, making the analysis rigorous and relevant.
    * **Systematic Methodology:** The design and implementation of MCP-SEC, the automated analysis framework, are well-explained and contribute to the paper's credibility. The framework is a useful tool for systematically assessing MCP deployments.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The large-scale analysis provides strong empirical evidence for the prevalence and diversity of exploitable vulnerabilities in the MCP ecosystem.
    * **Root Cause Analysis:** The identification of context-tool isolation and least-privilege enforcement as key architectural flaws is critical for understanding and addressing the problem.
    * **Practical Implications:** The paper directly addresses the urgent need for defense mechanisms in LLM-integrated environments and offers concrete defense strategies.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Real-World Attack Demonstrations:** While the paper theoretically demonstrates the feasibility of MCP-UPD and uses a motivating example, actual demonstrations of successful attacks against deployed MCP systems could strengthen the argument. This would also provide more detailed insight into the exact ways to exploit these vulnerabilities.
    * **Potential for False Negatives:** While the unanimous voting strategy minimizes false positives in tool capability analysis, it may also overlook potentially exploitable tools, leading to an underestimation of the attack surface. A sensitivity analysis varying the model parameters could provide more insight into this problem.
    * **Generalizability to Other Frameworks:** While the paper focuses on MCP, it is important to consider the potential impact of parasitic toolchain attacks on other LLM-tool integration frameworks and how these vulnerabilities might manifest differently.
    * **Defense mechanisms not directly evaluated:** The paper has defense mechanisms but doesn't provide data on the effectiveness of the same.

* **Potential Influence:**  This paper has the potential to significantly influence the security of LLM-integrated applications. By exposing a novel attack class and highlighting the architectural vulnerabilities of MCP, the paper can drive the development and deployment of more secure MCP implementations. It could influence future design choices in other LLM frameworks to address these challenges proactively.  It can also inform security guidelines and best practices for developers working with LLM-integrated systems.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by identifying and analyzing a new class of attacks within the emerging MCP ecosystem. The novelty of the threat model, combined with the thorough empirical evaluation, provides a strong argument for the importance of this work. The identification of root causes and proposed defense mechanisms offer practical guidance for improving the security of LLM-integrated applications. While the lack of real-world attack demonstrations and potential for false negatives in the tool analysis weaken the impact slightly, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses, making it a high-impact contribution to the field.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### From Rigging to Waving: 3D-Guided Diffusion for Natural Animation of Hand-Drawn Characters
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06573v1)
- **Authors**: Jie Zhou, Linzi Qu, Miu-Ling Lam, Hongbo Fu
- **Abstract**: Hand-drawn character animation is a vibrant field in computer graphics, presenting challenges in achieving geometric consistency while conveying expressive motion. Traditional skeletal animation methods maintain geometric consistency but struggle with complex non-rigid elements like flowing hair and skirts, leading to unnatural deformation. Conversely, video diffusion models synthesize realistic dynamics but often create geometric distortions in stylized drawings due to domain gaps. This work proposes a hybrid animation system that combines skeletal animation and video diffusion. Initially, coarse images are generated from characters retargeted with skeletal animations for geometric guidance. These images are then enhanced in texture and secondary dynamics using video diffusion priors, framing this enhancement as an inpainting task. A domain-adapted diffusion model refines user-masked regions needing improvement, especially for secondary dynamics. To enhance motion realism further, we introduce a Secondary Dynamics Injection (SDI) strategy in the denoising process, incorporating features from a pre-trained diffusion model enriched with human motion priors. Additionally, to tackle unnatural deformations from low-poly single-mesh character modeling, we present a Hair Layering Modeling (HLM) technique that uses segmentation maps to separate hair from the body, allowing for more natural animation of long-haired characters. Extensive experiments show that our system outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both quantitative and qualitative evaluations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel hybrid system for animating hand-drawn characters, combining the strengths of skeletal animation and video diffusion models. The core idea involves using skeletal animation to generate coarse, geometrically consistent images, which are then enhanced with texture details and secondary dynamics using a domain-adapted video diffusion model.  To address common issues, the paper proposes a Secondary Dynamics Injection (SDI) strategy to infuse human motion priors from a pre-trained diffusion model and a Hair Layering Modeling (HLM) technique to improve the animation of characters with complex hairstyles.  The authors demonstrate through experiments that their system outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both qualitative and quantitative evaluations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its clever integration of skeletal animation and video diffusion models. While both techniques are established, the specific way they're combined to address the limitations of each in the context of hand-drawn character animation is innovative. The introduction of SDI and HLM further contributes to the technical novelty. The concept of treating animation refinement as an inpainting problem guided by rendered 3D geometry also represents a fresh perspective.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a significant challenge in computer graphics: creating realistic and visually appealing animations of stylized characters. Current approaches often struggle to maintain geometric consistency while capturing subtle secondary motions. The proposed system provides a more robust solution by leveraging the strengths of both skeletal animation (geometric control) and video diffusion (motion priors).  A potential impact would be in making character animation accessible to users without specialized animation skills.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Hybrid Approach:** The synergistic combination of skeletal animation and video diffusion is a key strength.
    *   **Technical Contributions:** The SDI and HLM techniques are valuable contributions that specifically address limitations in previous approaches.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The paper presents a thorough experimental evaluation, including both quantitative metrics and qualitative comparisons to state-of-the-art methods.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed system and its underlying concepts. The illustrations and figures are effective in conveying the key ideas.
    * **User Friendly Editing:** The method offers an edit propagation feature, allowing the modification of the character's apperance in the reference image, automatically propogating it through the animation sequence.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Training Data:** While acknowledged, the reliance on a relatively small, synthesized training dataset is a limitation. This could restrict the generalizability of the domain-adapted diffusion model to a wider range of character styles and motions.
    *   **Pose Limitations:** The pre-trained human video diffusion model's inherent bias towards typical human motions could limit the system's ability to handle highly exaggerated or non-humanoid poses. The paper acknowledges this as a limitation.
    *   **Reliance on Manual Segmentation:** The HLM technique requires manual hair/body segmentation, which could be a bottleneck for users without specialized skills. An automatic segmentation approach would improve usability.
    *  **Lack of Facial Expression Control:** The framework does not support explicit facial expression control, which reduces expressive potential of the generated animations.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in character animation and video generation. The ideas presented could be extended to other animation styles and integrated into existing animation tools.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The method combines exisiting methods such as skeletal animation, diffusion based approaches along with new ones such as SDI and HLM to improve the performance of generating character animations. A limited training data could hinder the approach when it comes to more diverse and complicated character animations.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of character animation by effectively integrating skeletal animation and video diffusion. The SDI and HLM techniques are valuable additions that address specific challenges in animating hand-drawn characters. The experimental results demonstrate the superiority of the proposed system over state-of-the-art methods. However, the limitations regarding the training data size, pose limitations, and manual segmentation prevent a higher score. While the approach is innovative and shows promise, addressing these weaknesses in future work would further enhance its impact and potential for real-world applications.

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 7/10

### CausNVS: Autoregressive Multi-view Diffusion for Flexible 3D Novel View Synthesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06579v1)
- **Authors**: Xin Kong, Daniel Watson, Yannick Strümpler, Michael Niemeyer, Federico Tombari
- **Abstract**: Multi-view diffusion models have shown promise in 3D novel view synthesis, but most existing methods adopt a non-autoregressive formulation. This limits their applicability in world modeling, as they only support a fixed number of views and suffer from slow inference due to denoising all frames simultaneously. To address these limitations, we propose CausNVS, a multi-view diffusion model in an autoregressive setting, which supports arbitrary input-output view configurations and generates views sequentially. We train CausNVS with causal masking and per-frame noise, using pairwise-relative camera pose encodings (CaPE) for precise camera control. At inference time, we combine a spatially-aware sliding-window with key-value caching and noise conditioning augmentation to mitigate drift. Our experiments demonstrate that CausNVS supports a broad range of camera trajectories, enables flexible autoregressive novel view synthesis, and achieves consistently strong visual quality across diverse settings. Project page: https://kxhit.github.io/CausNVS.html.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "CausNVS: Autoregressive Multi-view Diffusion for Flexible 3D Novel View Synthesis".

**Summary**

The paper introduces CausNVS, an autoregressive multi-view diffusion model for novel view synthesis.  Unlike existing multi-view diffusion models which are non-causal and generate all target views simultaneously, CausNVS generates views sequentially, conditioning each new view on previously generated ones and the available input views. The key contributions include: 1) a causal formulation that supports arbitrary input/output view configurations, 2) a training scheme with per-frame noise conditioning to mitigate autoregressive drift, and 3) the use of pairwise-relative camera pose encodings (CaPE) for efficient inference with key-value caching, allowing for a spatially-aware sliding window attention mechanism.  Experiments demonstrate that CausNVS achieves competitive visual quality compared to state-of-the-art methods on standard benchmarks, while also enabling stable autoregressive rollouts and generalization to flexible view configurations.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a compelling combination of existing techniques to address a significant limitation in multi-view diffusion models.

    *   The autoregressive formulation for multi-view diffusion is a logical extension, but the paper tackles the practical challenges of autoregressive drift and KV caching incompatibility in a clever way.
    *   Per-frame noise conditioning and noise conditioning augmentation are adapted from existing literature but are applied effectively in the context of multi-view synthesis to address the drift issue.
    *   The adoption of CaPE for efficient KV caching in this context is also a notable contribution, allowing the model to maintain spatial consistency over long sequences.
    *   The integration of a spatially-aware sliding window is another practical enhancement that allows for scalable inference.

    While individual components are not entirely novel, the *integration* of these techniques to achieve flexible and stable autoregressive novel view synthesis is a valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:**

    *   The non-causal nature of existing multi-view diffusion models limits their application in interactive or streaming scenarios where views are requested sequentially. CausNVS addresses this limitation, opening up new possibilities for real-time or interactive 3D applications.
    *   The paper demonstrates the ability to handle arbitrary input and output view configurations within a single trained model, which is a significant improvement over methods that require separate training or padding for different configurations.
    *   The results show competitive performance compared to state-of-the-art methods on standard benchmarks, while also demonstrating superior generalization to flexible view configurations and stable autoregressive rollouts.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-explained technical details and justifications for design choices.
    *   Thorough experimental evaluation on standard benchmarks and with ablation studies.
    *   Qualitative results that highlight the advantages of the proposed approach.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the paper tackles the autoregressive drift problem, long-context generation remains a challenge, as indicated by the performance degradation on longer sequences.
    *   The model still relies on multi-step denoising, which limits its real-time applicability.
    *   The individual components (noise conditioning, CaPE) are not entirely novel, although their integration is effective.
    *   The paper mentions that no scale sweeping is applied. However, it is unclear if scale sweeping has any impact on the generalisability of the proposed approach.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper is likely to influence future research on multi-view diffusion models by demonstrating the feasibility and benefits of an autoregressive formulation.
    *   The techniques for mitigating autoregressive drift and enabling efficient inference with KV caching could be adopted in other sequential generation tasks.
    *   The paper provides a strong baseline for future research on flexible and scalable novel view synthesis.
*   **Rigor Justification**

    The paper demonstrates strong performance and offers a flexible approach for a critical computer vision problem. The authors present a convincing solution by cleverly integrating existing techniques. The paper is well-written and provides good justification for the authors' choices.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of novel view synthesis. While the individual components are not revolutionary, the *integration* of these components into a working autoregressive multi-view diffusion model is significant. The paper addresses a critical limitation of existing multi-view diffusion models and opens up new possibilities for real-time and interactive applications. The paper is well-written, thoroughly evaluated, and likely to influence future research in the field. The remaining weaknesses (limitations in long-context generation and real-time applicability) are directions for future research and do not significantly detract from the paper's overall contribution. For these reasons, the score assigned falls between 7 and 8.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLMs in Cybersecurity: Friend or Foe in the Human Decision Loop?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06595v1)
- **Authors**: Irdin Pekaric, Philipp Zech, Tom Mattson
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are transforming human decision-making by acting as cognitive collaborators. Yet, this promise comes with a paradox: while LLMs can improve accuracy, they may also erode independent reasoning, promote over-reliance and homogenize decisions. In this paper, we investigate how LLMs shape human judgment in security-critical contexts. Through two exploratory focus groups (unaided and LLM-supported), we assess decision accuracy, behavioral resilience and reliance dynamics. Our findings reveal that while LLMs enhance accuracy and consistency in routine decisions, they can inadvertently reduce cognitive diversity and improve automation bias, which is especially the case among users with lower resilience. In contrast, high-resilience individuals leverage LLMs more effectively, suggesting that cognitive traits mediate AI benefit.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the impact of Large Language Models (LLMs) on human decision-making in cybersecurity contexts. It investigates whether LLMs, acting as cognitive collaborators, enhance or hinder human judgment. Through exploratory focus groups (with and without LLM support), the study assesses decision accuracy, behavioral resilience, and reliance dynamics. The findings suggest that while LLMs can improve accuracy and consistency in routine tasks, they may also reduce cognitive diversity and induce automation bias, especially among users with lower resilience. Conversely, high-resilience individuals leverage LLMs more effectively.  The study advocates for LLM interfaces that adapt to users' cognitive profiles to mitigate risks and promote judgment diversity.

**Novelty and Significance Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a timely and relevant research question: the role of LLMs in human-in-the-loop cybersecurity decision-making. This is crucial as LLMs are rapidly being adopted in various domains, including security, raising concerns about their impact on human expertise and autonomy.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Empirical Investigation:** The paper provides valuable empirical evidence based on focus groups, which is a relatively novel approach in this specific area.
    *   **Resilience Focus:**  The integration of the "behavioral resilience" construct is a significant contribution.  It moves beyond simple accuracy metrics to consider the psychological factors influencing how users interact with and respond to AI assistance. This adds nuance to the understanding of human-AI collaboration.
    *   **Nuanced Findings:** The study doesn't simply conclude that LLMs are "good" or "bad."  Instead, it reveals that their impact is contingent on individual characteristics (resilience) and task complexity. This highlights the need for carefully designed human-AI interfaces.
    *   **Design Implications:** The paper offers concrete design implications for creating LLM interfaces that mitigate potential downsides and promote human judgment and diversity. These suggestions are actionable and directly address the research question.
    *   **Open-Source Data:** The data from the focus group are publicly available

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Sample Size:** The small sample size (N=21) and specific demographic (graduate students with cybersecurity background) limit the generalizability of the findings.  The conclusions might not hold for other populations (e.g., experienced security professionals, non-experts).
    *   **Focus Group limitations:** The reliance on focus groups, while providing rich qualitative data, is susceptible to biases and social dynamics.
    *   **Causality limitations:** The study shows correlations between resilience and performance, but it cannot establish causation.  It's possible that other factors (e.g., prior experience) are driving both resilience and effective LLM use.
    *   **Lack of Pure LLM Group:** The absence of a pure LLM group for comparison could have helped with stronger insights on LLMs performance with no human interference.

*   **Justification of Significance:**

    The paper's significance lies in its contribution to the emerging field of human-AI collaboration in security. While many studies focus on the technical aspects of LLMs, this paper provides a human-centric perspective, highlighting the behavioral and cognitive considerations that are often overlooked. The findings are relevant to practitioners, researchers, and policymakers involved in the design and deployment of LLM-based security systems. Understanding how LLMs affect human judgment is crucial for ensuring that these systems are used responsibly and effectively. The resilience framing is powerful and helps move the field past simple performance metrics.

**Conclusion:**

Despite the limitations related to sample size and scope, the paper makes a valuable contribution by providing empirical evidence and actionable design recommendations. The novel focus on resilience and the nuanced findings regarding the impact of LLMs on human judgment justify a solid rating.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### HAVE: Head-Adaptive Gating and ValuE Calibration for Hallucination Mitigation in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06596v1)
- **Authors**: Xin Tong, Zhi Lin, Jingya Wang, Bo Jin
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) often produce hallucinations in retrieval-augmented or long-context generation, even when relevant evidence is present. This stems from two issues: head importance is treated as input-agnostic, and raw attention weights poorly reflect each token's true contribution. We present HAVE (Head-Adaptive Gating and ValuE Calibration), a parameter-free decoding framework that directly addresses both challenges. HAVE introduces head-adaptive gating, which performs instance-level soft reweighing of attention heads, and value calibration, which augments attention with the magnitude of value vectors to approximate write-back contribution. Together, these modules construct token-level evidence aligned with model updates and fuse it with the LM distribution through a lightweight uncertainty-scaled policy. HAVE requires no finetuning and operates in a single forward pass, making it efficient and broadly applicable. Experiments across multiple QA benchmarks and LLM families demonstrate that HAVE consistently reduces hallucinations and outperforms strong baselines, including DAGCD, with modest overhead. The framework is transparent, reproducible, and readily integrates with off-the-shelf LLMs, advancing trustworthy generation in real-world settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "HAVE: HEAD-ADAPTIVE GATING AND VALUE CALIBRATION FOR HALLUCINATION MITIGATION IN LARGE LANGUAGE MODELS":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HAVE, a novel decoding framework designed to mitigate hallucinations in Large Language Models (LLMs) used in retrieval-augmented and long-context generation scenarios.  HAVE addresses two key issues: the static treatment of head importance and the poor correlation between raw attention weights and actual token contribution to the prediction. It achieves this through two main modules: Head-Adaptive Gating (HAG), which dynamically reweights attention heads based on input context, and Value Calibration (VC), which adjusts attention weights using value vector magnitudes to better approximate the influence of each token on the residual stream update. HAVE is parameter-free, efficient, and operates in a single forward pass. Experimental results on several QA benchmarks demonstrate that HAVE consistently reduces hallucinations and outperforms strong baseline methods. The paper emphasizes the interpretability of HAVE's internal signals, like dynamic head weights and calibrated token scores.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the specific combination of HAG and VC for hallucination mitigation. While attention-guided decoding and head selection have been explored previously, the adaptive, instance-level reweighting of attention heads coupled with value calibration offers a fresh perspective.  The idea of using value vector magnitudes to refine attention weights and better reflect actual contribution is a valuable contribution.  However, the components taken separately are less novel.  HAG can be seen as a form of dynamic attention masking or pruning, while VC is essentially a form of attention weight refinement. The *combination* and parameter-free nature are key differentiators.

*   **Significance:** The problem of hallucination in LLMs is a critical one, hindering their reliability and trustworthiness, especially in knowledge-intensive applications.  HAVE addresses this problem directly and provides a practical solution. The efficiency of HAVE (no fine-tuning, single forward pass) makes it highly deployable. The performance gains demonstrated across different QA benchmarks and LLM families suggest the general applicability of the framework. The increased interpretability of the model is also a notable contribution, as it provides insights into the model's reasoning process and allows for more targeted debugging. However, the gains aren't astronomical and in some instances, it only slightly outperforms existing methods, therefore somewhat limiting the significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Effective mitigation of hallucinations compared to strong baselines.
    *   Parameter-free and efficient design, allowing for easy integration with existing LLMs.
    *   Enhanced interpretability through dynamic head weights and calibrated token scores.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across multiple QA benchmarks and LLM families.
    *   Clear and well-written paper with a thorough description of the method and experiments.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The performance gains, while consistent, are not always substantial compared to the DAGCD baseline, especially on the SearchQA dataset, suggesting that it may be less effective on datasets with significant noise.
    *   The individual modules (HAG and VC) are not entirely novel when viewed in isolation, although their synergistic combination is innovative.
    *   The paper focuses on QA datasets and does not explore the effectiveness of HAVE in other generation tasks, such as summarization or dialogue. The lack of comparison to more recent and advanced baselines further limits the significance of this study.

*   **Potential Impact:** HAVE offers a practical and effective approach to mitigate hallucinations in LLMs, and it could be readily adopted by researchers and practitioners working with LLMs in retrieval-augmented and long-context settings. Its interpretability also makes it a valuable tool for understanding and debugging LLMs. The techniques described in the paper could inspire further research on attention mechanisms and decoding strategies for improving the reliability and trustworthiness of LLMs.

**Justification for the Score:**

While HAVE presents a valuable combination of techniques and offers a practical solution for hallucination mitigation, the incremental nature of the individual modules and the somewhat limited performance gains warrant a more conservative score. The consistency of the improvements, the ease of implementation, and the increased interpretability boost the score. Compared to the state-of-the-art, the significance of this approach remains questionable and future improvements are encouraged.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Guided Decoding and Its Critical Role in Retrieval-Augmented Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06631v1)
- **Authors**: Özgür Uğur, Musa Yılmaz, Esra Şavirdi, Özay Ezerceli, Mahmut El Huseyni, Selva Taş, Reyhan Bayraktar
- **Abstract**: The integration of Large Language Models (LLMs) into various applications has driven the need for structured and reliable responses. A key challenge in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems is ensuring that outputs align with expected formats while minimizing hallucinations. This study examines the role of guided decoding in RAG systems, comparing three methods, Outlines, XGrammar, and LM Format Enforcer, across different multi-turn prompting setups (0-turn, 1-turn, and 2-turn). By evaluating success rates, hallucination rates, and output quality, we provide insights into their performance and applicability. Our findings reveal how multi-turn interactions influence guided decoding, uncovering unexpected performance variations that can inform method selection for specific use cases. This work advances the understanding of structured output generation in RAG systems, offering both theoretical insights and practical guidance for LLM deployment.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper.

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the role of guided decoding in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems, focusing on structured output generation. It compares three guided decoding methods - Outlines, XGrammar, and LM Format Enforcer - across different multi-turn conversational setups (0-turn, 1-turn, and 2-turn). The authors evaluate these methods based on success rates, hallucination rates (particularly false positive rates of citing incorrect documents), and overall output quality, using the Qwen2.5-72B-Instruct and LLaMA-3.3-70B-Instruct models.  The study analyzes how multi-turn interactions influence the effectiveness of each guided decoding method and identifies performance variations that can guide method selection for specific use cases. The paper highlights the trade-offs between the different methods regarding flexibility, enforcement, and computational efficiency. The overall conclusion is that guided decoding is essential for creating reliable RAG-based applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:*

The paper's novelty is mixed. While guided decoding in LLMs and RAG is not entirely new, the comparative analysis of *specific* guided decoding methods (Outlines, XGrammar, and LM Format Enforcer) across *different multi-turn conversational settings* is a valuable and relatively novel contribution.  Previous work often focuses on one method in isolation or doesn't explicitly examine the influence of multi-turn prompts in a RAG setting with such a comparative focus.  The investigation into how each decoding method's performance shifts across varying turn numbers provides valuable empirical data and informs practical application. The observation that specific method performance (especially regarding hallucination rates) changes with the number of turns offers a refined understanding beyond typical zero-shot evaluations.

*Significance:*

The paper's significance lies in its practical implications for deploying LLMs in real-world RAG applications. The findings provide actionable insights for choosing the appropriate guided decoding method based on the desired trade-offs between strictness, flexibility, and computational cost, considering the specific prompt setup, especially regarding multi-turn conversations. Specifically, understanding which methods work best for few-shot prompting or zero-shot settings, and how their behavior changes in longer conversational contexts, is important.  The practical insights regarding complex Turkish legal documents enhance the paper's significance. By emphasizing the importance of reference accuracy in RAG (and minimizing hallucinations), the paper addresses a crucial aspect of reliable knowledge-intensive applications.

*Strengths:*

*   **Clear Experimental Setup:** The paper outlines the experiment setup, including models used, and datasets.
*   **Comparative Analysis:** The comparative evaluation is a major strength, helping to identify the strengths and weaknesses of each method.
*   **Focus on Hallucinations:** Explicitly evaluating hallucination rates is crucial for RAG systems, highlighting the importance of factuality.
*   **Practical Implications:** The paper offers valuable guidance for practitioners on how to select the most suitable guided decoding method.
*   **Consideration of Multi-turn Interactions:** The multi-turn analysis adds depth and realism to the evaluation.
*   **Clear writing and organization**

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited Dataset Transparency:** While a dataset is mentioned, full accessibility is restricted for a portion of it. Open access to *all* data would enhance the reproducibility and impact of the findings.
*   **Model Selection:** The study relies on only two base LLMs. Expanding the models tested would improve the generalizability of the results. It would be beneficial to see how these methods perform with smaller or larger LLMs, as well as with different architectures.
