### BIR-Adapter: A Low-Complexity Diffusion Model Adapter for Blind Image Restoration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06904v1)
- **Authors**: Cem Eteke, Alexander Griessel, Wolfgang Kellerer, Eckehard Steinbach
- **Abstract**: This paper introduces BIR-Adapter, a low-complexity blind image restoration adapter for diffusion models. The BIR-Adapter enables the utilization of the prior of pre-trained large-scale diffusion models on blind image restoration without training any auxiliary feature extractor. We take advantage of the robustness of pretrained models. We extract features from degraded images via the model itself and extend the self-attention mechanism with these degraded features. We introduce a sampling guidance mechanism to reduce hallucinations. We perform experiments on synthetic and real-world degradations and demonstrate that BIR-Adapter achieves competitive or better performance compared to state-of-the-art methods while having significantly lower complexity. Additionally, its adapter-based design enables integration into other diffusion models, enabling broader applications in image restoration tasks. We showcase this by extending a super-resolution-only model to perform better under additional unknown degradations.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "BIR-Adapter: A Low-Complexity Diffusion Model Adapter for Blind Image Restoration."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BIR-Adapter, a novel adapter for blind image restoration using pre-trained large-scale diffusion models. Instead of training a completely new model or fine-tuning a large cloned model (like in ControlNet), BIR-Adapter leverages the robustness of existing pre-trained diffusion models by extracting features directly from the degraded input image within the model itself. These features are then incorporated into the self-attention mechanism to guide the restoration process. Furthermore, a sampling guidance mechanism is introduced to mitigate hallucinations during the image generation process. The authors demonstrate that BIR-Adapter achieves competitive or superior performance compared to state-of-the-art methods while significantly reducing model complexity (i.e., the number of trainable parameters). The paper also highlights the plug-and-play nature of the adapter by integrating it into a super-resolution-only model to enhance its performance under unknown degradations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in extracting degraded features from the diffusion model *itself* and injecting these back into the self-attention mechanism. This is a more efficient approach compared to training separate feature extractors or fine-tuning a complete clone of the diffusion model. The guided sampling mechanism for hallucination reduction is also a useful contribution, though it is a relatively standard technique in diffusion models. The core idea of using robust features from intermediate layers of a diffusion model is novel.

*   **Significance:** The reduced complexity of the BIR-Adapter is a significant advantage. It allows for efficient adaptation of powerful diffusion models to image restoration tasks without the heavy computational burden of training a new model from scratch or fine-tuning a full-sized clone. The demonstrated plug-and-play ability also increases its potential impact, enabling researchers to easily integrate the adapter into existing diffusion model architectures. The plug-and-play aspect and the lower parameter count hold promise for broader adoption.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Low Complexity:** The primary strength is the significant reduction in trainable parameters compared to methods like DiffBIR or fine-tuning with ControlNet. This makes the approach more accessible and computationally efficient.
    *   **Plug-and-Play:**  Demonstrating the adapter's ability to be integrated into different diffusion models, specifically a super-resolution model, showcases its versatility.
    *   **Competitive Performance:** Achieving competitive or better results than state-of-the-art methods on standard benchmarks is a key strength. The results in Tab. 3 suggest the superiority of the method to other state-of-the-art models in many cases, particularly with certain forms of degradation.
    *   **Hallucination Mitigation:**  The use of guided sampling is effective in reducing the prevalence of artifacts and ensuring high-quality output.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Pre-trained Models:** The approach is heavily reliant on the quality and prior knowledge encoded in the pre-trained diffusion model. If the pre-trained model does not generalize well to certain degradation types or image domains, the BIR-Adapter might struggle.
    *   **Parameter Tuning:** While the number of trainable parameters is significantly reduced, it is still not clear how to choose a good subset of layers from where to select the degraded features, or optimal values for hyperparameters such as the guidance threshold.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** The ablation experiments could be more thorough. The current ablations do not fully analyze the importance of various components of the BIR-Adapter.

* **Limitations discussed:**
    The paper highlights several limitations including (1) Reliance on high frequency details from initial restoration step, (2) choice of value for guidance parameter for effective reduction of hallucination artifacts.

*   **Potential Influence:** The BIR-Adapter provides a practical approach to adapt diffusion models for image restoration. The low complexity and plug-and-play nature can make diffusion-based image restoration more accessible to a wider range of researchers and practitioners, potentially driving further innovation in this area.

**Justification for the Score:**

I'm assigning a score of 7. The paper demonstrates a clear improvement in efficiency for blind image restoration through the introduction of the BIR-Adapter. The plug-and-play nature is important for its wider applicability and adoption. However, the reliance on pre-trained models, the tuning of layer selection for degraded features and guidance parameter value remain potential limitations that somewhat restrict its scope and robustness. Additional exploration of the method's performance on more varied and challenging datasets, combined with a more in-depth parameter analysis, could significantly strengthen the paper's contribution.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Staying in the Sweet Spot: Responsive Reasoning Evolution via Capability-Adaptive Hint Scaffolding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06923v1)
- **Authors**: Ziheng Li, Zexu Sun, Jinman Zhao, Erxue Min, Yongcheng Zeng, Hui Wu, Hengyi Cai, Shuaiqiang Wang, Dawei Yin, Xu Chen, Zhi-Hong Deng
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has achieved remarkable success in enhancing the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). However, existing RLVR methods often suffer from exploration inefficiency due to mismatches between the training data's difficulty and the model's capability. LLMs fail to discover viable reasoning paths when problems are overly difficult, while learning little new capability when problems are too simple. In this work, we formalize the impact of problem difficulty by quantifying the relationship between loss descent speed and rollout accuracy. Building on this analysis, we propose SEELE, a novel supervision-aided RLVR framework that dynamically adjusts problem difficulty to stay within the high-efficiency region. SEELE augments each training sample by appending a hint (part of a full solution) after the original problem. Unlike previous hint-based approaches, SEELE deliberately and adaptively adjusts the hint length for each problem to achieve an optimal difficulty. To determine the optimal hint length, SEELE employs a multi-round rollout sampling strategy. In each round, it fits an item response theory model to the accuracy-hint pairs collected in preceding rounds to predict the required hint length for the next round. This instance-level, real-time difficulty adjustment aligns problem difficulty with the evolving model capability, thereby improving exploration efficiency. Experimental results show that SEELE outperforms Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) and Supervised Fine-tuning (SFT) by +11.8 and +10.5 points, respectively, and surpasses the best previous supervision-aided approach by +3.6 points on average across six math reasoning benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SEELE, a novel reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) framework designed to enhance the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). SEELE addresses the challenge of exploration inefficiency in existing RLVR methods, which often suffer from a mismatch between problem difficulty and model capability. SEELE works by dynamically adjusting the difficulty of training samples through capability-adaptive hint scaffolding. Specifically, it appends a partial solution (hint) to each problem and adaptively adjusts the hint length to maintain an optimal level of difficulty. This optimal difficulty is determined using a multi-round rollout sampling strategy and an item response theory model to predict the required hint length. The authors demonstrate that SEELE outperforms existing RLVR methods on several math and general reasoning benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its **dynamic and instance-level approach to hint scaffolding** within the RLVR framework. Unlike prior hint-based methods that apply static or indiscriminate guidance, SEELE utilizes real-time feedback and accuracy prediction to tailor hint length to the specific problem and evolving model capability. This addresses a key limitation of previous approaches. The integration of Item Response Theory to predict accuracy based on hinting rate also seems reasonably novel in this application.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to improve the **efficiency and effectiveness of RLVR training** for LLMs. By dynamically aligning problem difficulty with model capability, SEELE can accelerate learning and enable LLMs to acquire more complex reasoning skills. The empirical results support this claim, demonstrating improved performance across various benchmarks. The theoretical analysis linking learning efficiency and rollout accuracy also provides a useful foundation for understanding RLVR dynamics. The claim of solving a relevant practical issue makes the findings useful for future applications of RLVR in LLMs.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the problem of exploration inefficiency in existing RLVR methods.
*   **Theoretically grounded:** The authors provide a theoretical analysis that supports their approach and offers insights into the relationship between problem difficulty and learning efficiency.
*   **Technically sound:** The proposed framework is well-designed and incorporates appropriate techniques (e.g., multi-round rollout sampling, item response theory).
*   **Strong empirical results:** The paper presents convincing experimental results that demonstrate the effectiveness of SEELE compared to existing methods.
*   **Reproducible:** The paper provides the repository, which helps for reproducibility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Complexity:** The framework involves several components, making it somewhat complex to implement.
*   **Reliance on expert annotation:** The method still relies on having access to ground-truth solutions to generate hints, which might be a limitation in some domains. Although the model itself is not trained to memorize these solutions, this reliance on data can be a hurdle.
*   **Limited generalizability claims:** Claims about better generalizability seem inflated, because the experiments showing them are very limited.
*   **Clarity**: The paper lacks clarity, and the results aren't easily and immediately understood.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research in RLVR for LLMs by providing a more effective approach to exploration and data curation. The idea of dynamically adapting problem difficulty could be applied in other areas of machine learning as well.

**Justification for the Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7/10. The paper presents a reasonably novel and significant contribution to the field of RLVR for LLMs by addressing the crucial issue of exploration inefficiency. The proposed SEELE framework is well-designed, theoretically grounded, and empirically validated. While the reliance on ground truth can be a limiting factor, the overall potential to improve RLVR training efficiency is substantial. However, the lack of clarity makes results harder to interpret.

**Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLaDA-VLA: Vision Language Diffusion Action Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06932v1)
- **Authors**: Yuqing Wen, Hebei Li, Kefan Gu, Yucheng Zhao, Tiancai Wang, Xiaoyan Sun
- **Abstract**: The rapid progress of auto-regressive vision-language models (VLMs) has inspired growing interest in vision-language-action models (VLA) for robotic manipulation. Recently, masked diffusion models, a paradigm distinct from autoregressive models, have begun to demonstrate competitive performance in text generation and multimodal applications, leading to the development of a series of diffusion-based VLMs (d-VLMs). However, leveraging such models for robot policy learning remains largely unexplored. In this work, we present LLaDA-VLA, the first Vision-Language-Diffusion-Action model built upon pretrained d-VLMs for robotic manipulation. To effectively adapt d-VLMs to robotic domain, we introduce two key designs: (1) a localized special-token classification strategy that replaces full-vocabulary classification with special action token classification, reducing adaptation difficulty; (2) a hierarchical action-structured decoding strategy that decodes action sequences hierarchically considering the dependencies within and across actions. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LLaDA-VLA significantly outperforms state-of-the-art VLAs on both simulation and real-world robots.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LLaDA-VLA: Vision Language Diffusion Action Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LLaDA-VLA, a novel vision-language-action model (VLA) that uses a diffusion model (d-VLM) as its core component. This contrasts with existing VLAs, which primarily rely on autoregressive models. The authors address challenges in adapting d-VLMs to robotic tasks by proposing two key innovations: a localized special-token classification strategy to reduce the domain gap between d-VLMs and robotic environments, and a hierarchical action-structured decoding strategy to explicitly model dependencies within and across actions.  Experiments demonstrate LLaDA-VLA's superior performance compared to state-of-the-art VLAs in both simulation and real-world robotic manipulation tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key strength of this paper lies in its pioneering effort to apply diffusion models to the VLA domain.  While diffusion models have gained traction in language generation and vision-language tasks, their application to action generation, especially in robotics, is relatively unexplored. The localized special-token classification and hierarchical action-structured decoding strategies are crucial for effectively adapting d-VLMs to the specific challenges of robotic control. These are not simply trivial adaptations but are thoughtful designs grounded in understanding the unique requirements of action generation. The authors explicitly address the limitations of direct application of diffusion models, offering a targeted solution.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is multi-faceted. First, it broadens the landscape of VLA models, offering a competitive alternative to the dominant autoregressive paradigm. The superior performance demonstrated by LLaDA-VLA suggests that diffusion models hold considerable promise for robotic applications. Second, the proposed techniques for domain adaptation and structured decoding can be readily adopted and extended in future research. Finally, the comprehensive experimental evaluation on various simulated and real-world environments provides a strong empirical foundation for the model's efficacy and generalization capabilities.  It provides convincing evidence of the potential of diffusion models in robot policy learning.
*   **Strengths:** The paper is well-written and clearly articulates the motivations, designs, and experimental results. The problem is well-defined, and the proposed solutions are technically sound and innovative. The experimental setup is comprehensive and includes comparisons with strong baselines on diverse benchmarks. The inclusion of real-world robot experiments adds significant value and demonstrates the practical relevance of the work. The ablation study thoroughly examines the contribution of each component of the proposed framework.
*   **Weaknesses:**  While the paper demonstrates strong results, a potential limitation is the reliance on a fixed-length output setting for action sequences. This may not be suitable for tasks that require variable-length action plans. Also, while the qualitative results are promising, more in-depth analysis of the generated action trajectories (e.g., smoothness, efficiency) would further strengthen the paper. While the action space is discretized, a discussion on the trade-offs associated with bin size would provide more insight. The comparison to other diffusion-based imitation methods is somewhat lacking, it is compared to methods that are purely autoregressive-based models.
*   **Potential Impact:** This work has the potential to inspire new research directions in VLA modeling, particularly in exploring the benefits of diffusion models for complex robotic tasks. The proposed techniques could be extended to other robotic domains and adapted to different types of diffusion models.  The success of LLaDA-VLA will likely encourage further investigation into diffusion-based approaches for robot learning and control.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty in applying diffusion models to VLAs and the significant performance gains demonstrated by LLaDA-VLA, combined with well-thought-out solutions to robotic-specific challenges, I believe a score of 8 is justified. The paper makes a substantial contribution to the field by introducing a new paradigm for VLA modeling and offering valuable insights into how to effectively adapt diffusion models to robotic manipulation tasks.  While the paper has some limitations in terms of trajectory analysis and a stronger analysis of diffusion methods, its pioneering effort and significant impact potential outweigh these weaknesses.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 8/10

### From Noise to Narrative: Tracing the Origins of Hallucinations in Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06938v1)
- **Authors**: Praneet Suresh, Jack Stanley, Sonia Joseph, Luca Scimeca, Danilo Bzdok
- **Abstract**: As generative AI systems become competent and democratized in science, business, and government, deeper insight into their failure modes now poses an acute need. The occasional volatility in their behavior, such as the propensity of transformer models to hallucinate, impedes trust and adoption of emerging AI solutions in high-stakes areas. In the present work, we establish how and when hallucinations arise in pre-trained transformer models through concept representations captured by sparse autoencoders, under scenarios with experimentally controlled uncertainty in the input space. Our systematic experiments reveal that the number of semantic concepts used by the transformer model grows as the input information becomes increasingly unstructured. In the face of growing uncertainty in the input space, the transformer model becomes prone to activate coherent yet input-insensitive semantic features, leading to hallucinated output. At its extreme, for pure-noise inputs, we identify a wide variety of robustly triggered and meaningful concepts in the intermediate activations of pre-trained transformer models, whose functional integrity we confirm through targeted steering. We also show that hallucinations in the output of a transformer model can be reliably predicted from the concept patterns embedded in transformer layer activations. This collection of insights on transformer internal processing mechanics has immediate consequences for aligning AI models with human values, AI safety, opening the attack surface for potential adversarial attacks, and providing a basis for automatic quantification of a model's hallucination risk.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper "From Noise to Narrative: Tracing the Origins of Hallucinations in Transformers":

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the origins of hallucinations in pre-trained transformer models by examining concept representations learned by sparse autoencoders (SAEs) under varying input uncertainty. The authors demonstrate that transformers tend to impose semantic structure even on noisy or random inputs, activating coherent and steerable concepts.  As input uncertainty increases, the model activates a wider range of semantic concepts, particularly in the middle layers, leading to hallucinated outputs.  The study further shows that the pattern of concept activations can reliably predict the occurrence of hallucinations, suggesting a measurable link between internal feature recruitment and output fidelity.  The authors conduct experiments on both vision and language transformers.

**Novelty and Significance Evaluation:**

This paper addresses a crucial problem in the field of AI: the "black box" nature of large language and vision models and their propensity to generate incorrect or misleading information (hallucinations). While many works focus on benchmarking hallucination rates, this paper delves into *why* hallucinations occur at the mechanistic level. The approach of using SAEs to probe the internal representations of transformers under controlled input conditions (noise, shuffled data) is novel and insightful.

**Strengths:**

*   **Mechanistic Interpretability:** The paper contributes significantly to the growing field of mechanistic interpretability. By using SAEs, the authors uncover meaningful concepts that are activated within the transformer layers, providing a human-understandable view of internal processing.
*   **Systematic Experiments:** The experiments are well-designed and systematic, varying the input uncertainty and data modality to establish the core findings. The experiments with noisy and shuffled inputs, in particular, are effective in revealing the model's inductive biases.
*   **Cross-Modal Validation:** Conducting experiments on both vision and language transformers strengthens the generalizability of the conclusions. The observation that both types of models exhibit similar behavior regarding concept activation and hallucination suggests that the findings are not specific to a particular architecture or data type.
*   **Predictive Power:** The finding that concept activation patterns can predict hallucinations is a significant step forward. This opens the door for developing techniques to detect and mitigate hallucinations in real-time.
*   **Potential Applications:** The insights gained from this research have a wide range of potential applications, including:
    *   Improving AI safety and reliability
    *   Developing techniques for aligning AI models with human values
    *   Opening new attack surfaces for adversarial attacks
    *   Quantifying a model's hallucination risk.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Scale:** The paper primarily focuses on relatively smaller models (ViT-B/32, Pythia-160m). While the findings are compelling, it remains an open question how well they will scale to the largest, state-of-the-art models (e.g., models with 100+ billion parameters). The study does include some analysis with Gemma 2B, which strengthens the scaling argument somewhat.
*   **Interpretability Limitations:** While SAEs provide a degree of interpretability, the learned concepts may still not fully capture the complexity of transformer internal processing. Some aspects of the model's decision-making process may remain opaque.
*   **Causality:** The paper establishes a correlation between concept activation patterns and hallucinations, but fully establishing causality is challenging. While concept suppression experiments provide evidence for a causal link, further work is needed to confirm this.
*   **Simplification:**  Hallucination is a complex phenomenon. Reducing it primarily to the activation of specific high-level concepts might be an oversimplification.  There may be other factors at play, such as the model's training data, architecture details, and decoding process.

**Significance:**

Despite these limitations, the paper represents a significant contribution to understanding the inner workings of transformer models and the origins of hallucinations. It provides a valuable framework for future research in this area. The findings have the potential to guide the development of more reliable, trustworthy, and aligned AI systems.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** This paper makes a novel and significant contribution to the field by offering a mechanistic explanation for hallucinations in transformer models. The use of SAEs to probe internal representations is insightful, and the experiments are well-designed. The potential applications of this research are broad and impactful. While some limitations remain (especially regarding scalability and full causal understanding), the paper provides a valuable foundation for future work and earns a score of 7.5 due to its novel approach and potential influence on the field.  The paper goes beyond simple benchmarking and offers a *why* behind a critical problem.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Outcome-based Exploration for LLM Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06941v1)
- **Authors**: Yuda Song, Julia Kempe, Remi Munos
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a powerful method for improving the reasoning abilities of large language models (LLMs). Outcome-based RL, which rewards policies solely for the correctness of the final answer, yields substantial accuracy gains but also induces a systematic loss in generation diversity. This collapse undermines real-world performance, where diversity is critical for test-time scaling. We analyze this phenomenon by viewing RL post-training as a sampling process and show that, strikingly, RL can reduce effective diversity even on the training set relative to the base model. Our study highlights two central findings: (i) a transfer of diversity degradation, where reduced diversity on solved problems propagates to unsolved ones, and (ii) the tractability of the outcome space, since reasoning tasks admit only a limited set of distinct answers. Motivated by these insights, we propose outcome-based exploration, which assigns exploration bonuses according to final outcomes. We introduce two complementary algorithms: historical exploration, which encourages rarely observed answers via UCB-style bonuses, and batch exploration, which penalizes within-batch repetition to promote test-time diversity. Experiments on standard competition math with Llama and Qwen models demonstrate that both methods improve accuracy while mitigating diversity collapse. On the theoretical side, we formalize the benefit of outcome-based exploration through a new model of outcome-based bandits. Together, these contributions chart a practical path toward RL methods that enhance reasoning without sacrificing the diversity essential for scalable deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Outcome-based Exploration for LLM Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the diversity collapse issue in reinforcement learning (RL) applied to large language model (LLM) reasoning.  RL, particularly when rewarding only the correctness of the final answer (outcome-based RL), can lead to significant accuracy gains but reduces the diversity of generated reasoning paths and answers. This lack of diversity hinders real-world performance, particularly when using techniques like test-time scaling.  The paper analyzes this phenomenon, revealing that diversity degradation occurs not just at test time but also during training (transfer of diversity degradation) and is exacerbated by the tractability of the outcome space (limited number of distinct answers).  To mitigate this, the authors propose outcome-based exploration methods, specifically "historical exploration" (UCB-style bonuses for rarely observed answers) and "batch exploration" (penalizing within-batch repetition). Experiments on math reasoning tasks using Llama and Qwen models demonstrate that these exploration methods improve accuracy while preserving or enhancing diversity. A theoretical analysis formalizes the benefit of outcome-based exploration through a novel model of outcome-based bandits.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific focus on outcome-based RL in the context of LLM reasoning. While exploration techniques in RL are well-established, the detailed analysis of diversity collapse in this particular setting, the identification of transfer of diversity degradation, and the concept of tractability of the outcome space are valuable contributions. The introduction of batch exploration, explicitly targeting test-time diversity, also represents a novel addition. The theoretical outcome-based bandit model provides a helpful theoretical framework. However, adapting UCB to LLM exploration isn't entirely new (though the nuances of outcome-based and batch-based exploration are unique).

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in deploying RL-trained LLMs: the trade-off between accuracy and diversity. The diversity collapse problem has significant practical implications, as it limits the benefits of test-time scaling, which relies on generating diverse options. The proposed methods offer a practical path towards more robust and scalable LLM reasoning systems. The experiments on standard benchmarks add weight to the practicality of the algorithms.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper articulates the diversity collapse problem very clearly and provides compelling empirical evidence.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The analysis of the training dynamics, particularly the "transfer of diversity degradation," offers a valuable insight.
    *   **Practical Solutions:** The proposed algorithms are relatively straightforward to implement and demonstrate tangible improvements in experiments.
    *   **Theoretical Grounding:** The outcome-based bandit model provides a theoretical justification for the proposed methods.
    *   **Comprehensive experiments:** Using multiple models and datasets supports the generalizability of the claims.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The focus on verifiable reasoning domains restricts the generalizability of the results. The algorithms might not directly apply to tasks where evaluating outcome correctness is difficult or impossible.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the proposed methods show improvements, the magnitude of these gains isn't always substantial, especially for the harder datasets.
    *   **Simplicity of Algorithms:** The algorithms, while effective, are conceptually relatively simple adaptations of existing RL techniques. The core idea is to leverage the limited outcome space of these tasks.
    *   **Hyperparameter Tuning:** The sensitivity to hyperparameters, especially the exploration coefficient 'c' and the baseline 'bo,' is a potential concern. More robust and adaptive methods might be desirable.
    *   **Theoretical Assumptions:** The outcome-based bandit analysis relies on strong assumptions (e.g., strong generalization), which may not fully hold in practice. A more nuanced theoretical framework could be beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in RL-based LLM training, particularly in reasoning tasks. The concepts of "transfer of diversity degradation" and "outcome-based exploration" provide a valuable lens for understanding and addressing the diversity collapse problem. The practical algorithms offer a useful starting point for developing more robust and scalable LLM reasoning systems.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by clearly identifying and analyzing a critical problem in RL-based LLM reasoning (diversity collapse). The proposed outcome-based exploration methods, while building upon existing RL techniques, are tailored to the specific characteristics of this setting and demonstrate practical improvements. The theoretical analysis, while relying on strong assumptions, provides a helpful formalization of the benefits of outcome-based exploration. However, the limited scope (verifiable domains), the incremental nature of the improvements, and the sensitivity to hyperparameters prevent a higher score. A more significant theoretical contribution or a broader set of experiments would be needed to justify a rating in the 8-10 range.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Directly Aligning the Full Diffusion Trajectory with Fine-Grained Human Preference
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06942v1)
- **Authors**: Xiangwei Shen, Zhimin Li, Zhantao Yang, Shiyi Zhang, Yingfang Zhang, Donghao Li, Chunyu Wang, Qinglin Lu, Yansong Tang
- **Abstract**: Recent studies have demonstrated the effectiveness of directly aligning diffusion models with human preferences using differentiable reward. However, they exhibit two primary challenges: (1) they rely on multistep denoising with gradient computation for reward scoring, which is computationally expensive, thus restricting optimization to only a few diffusion steps; (2) they often need continuous offline adaptation of reward models in order to achieve desired aesthetic quality, such as photorealism or precise lighting effects. To address the limitation of multistep denoising, we propose Direct-Align, a method that predefines a noise prior to effectively recover original images from any time steps via interpolation, leveraging the equation that diffusion states are interpolations between noise and target images, which effectively avoids over-optimization in late timesteps. Furthermore, we introduce Semantic Relative Preference Optimization (SRPO), in which rewards are formulated as text-conditioned signals. This approach enables online adjustment of rewards in response to positive and negative prompt augmentation, thereby reducing the reliance on offline reward fine-tuning. By fine-tuning the FLUX.1.dev model with optimized denoising and online reward adjustment, we improve its human-evaluated realism and aesthetic quality by over 3x.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new method called Semantic Relative Preference Optimization (SRPO) to improve the alignment of text-to-image diffusion models with human preferences. SRPO addresses two main limitations of existing online reinforcement learning approaches: the restriction to a few diffusion steps due to computational cost, and the lack of online mechanisms to adjust rewards for desired aesthetic qualities (e.g., photorealism, lighting). SRPO achieves this through two key components: 1) Direct-Align, which predefines a noise prior allowing image recovery from any timestep, and 2) Text-Conditional Reward Modification, formulating rewards as text-conditioned signals.  By combining these innovations, the method enables efficient training, reduces reward hacking, allows for online reward adjustments via prompt augmentation, and results in enhanced realism and aesthetic quality.  The method is evaluated on the FLUX.1-dev model, demonstrating substantial improvements compared to baselines and state-of-the-art online RL methods, including a significant increase in human-evaluated realism and aesthetic quality while requiring only 10 minutes of training on 32 NVIDIA H20 GPUs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper introduces several novel components. The Direct-Align method is a clever workaround to the computational bottleneck of multi-step denoising in reward scoring. Injecting noise as a prior and recovering from any given timestep allows for more efficient exploration of the diffusion trajectory and mitigation of early timestep issues. The Semantic Relative Preference Optimization using text-conditional signals to adjust rewards is also innovative, offering a way to dynamically adapt to the desired aesthetic quality without retraining the reward model or relying on costly offline preparations. However, using text-conditioning for reward adjustments isn't entirely new, so this component is an incremental improvement.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical and well-recognized problem: aligning generative models with human preferences, a task that directly impacts the usability and practical value of these models.  The gains in training efficiency are substantial, allowing high-quality results with minimal computational resources. The improvement in human-evaluated realism is a significant step forward, as many existing RL methods struggle to achieve photorealism and can introduce unwanted artifacts.  The online reward adjustment mechanism offers a powerful tool for fine-grained control over the generation process.

* **Strengths:**
    * The method is well-motivated, addressing clear limitations in prior art.
    * The technical approach is innovative and combines several ideas in a cohesive manner.
    * The experimental results are comprehensive, using a diverse set of evaluation metrics and human assessments.
    * The improvement in training efficiency is impressive.
    * The online reward adjustment feature provides a practical tool for controlling aesthetic attributes.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * While novel, some aspects, such as using text embeddings in reward functions, build on existing ideas, diminishing the radicalness of the work.
    * There isn't a thorough discussion of how specific control words were chosen, potentially limiting the reproducibility or generalizability of results.
    * The results are presented primarily on a single base model (FLUX.1-dev). Demonstrating similar results on other architectures would strengthen the claims.
    * While efficient, the 10-minute training time still necessitates significant computational resources (32 NVIDIA H20 GPUs), which might not be accessible to all researchers or practitioners.

* **Impact:**  The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field. The improvements in training efficiency, realism, and control could enable more widespread adoption of RL-based alignment techniques for text-to-image models. The online reward adjustment mechanism opens new avenues for interactive control and customization of generative models. The techniques are likely to be relevant to other generative modeling tasks, such as video generation.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a significant advance in the field of aligning text-to-image diffusion models with human preferences. While not revolutionary in every component, it creatively combines existing ideas and introduces novel techniques that result in substantial improvements in training efficiency, image quality, and control. The weaknesses are relatively minor, and the paper's impact on the field is likely to be considerable.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Interleaving Reasoning for Better Text-to-Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06945v1)
- **Authors**: Wenxuan Huang, Shuang Chen, Zheyong Xie, Shaosheng Cao, Shixiang Tang, Yufan Shen, Qingyu Yin, Wenbo Hu, Xiaoman Wang, Yuntian Tang, Junbo Qiao, Yue Guo, Yao Hu, Zhenfei Yin, Philip Torr, Yu Cheng, Wanli Ouyang, Shaohui Lin
- **Abstract**: Unified multimodal understanding and generation models recently have achieve significant improvement in image generation capability, yet a large gap remains in instruction following and detail preservation compared to systems that tightly couple comprehension with generation such as GPT-4o. Motivated by recent advances in interleaving reasoning, we explore whether such reasoning can further improve Text-to-Image (T2I) generation. We introduce Interleaving Reasoning Generation (IRG), a framework that alternates between text-based thinking and image synthesis: the model first produces a text-based thinking to guide an initial image, then reflects on the result to refine fine-grained details, visual quality, and aesthetics while preserving semantics. To train IRG effectively, we propose Interleaving Reasoning Generation Learning (IRGL), which targets two sub-goals: (1) strengthening the initial think-and-generate stage to establish core content and base quality, and (2) enabling high-quality textual reflection and faithful implementation of those refinements in a subsequent image. We curate IRGL-300K, a dataset organized into six decomposed learning modes that jointly cover learning text-based thinking, and full thinking-image trajectories. Starting from a unified foundation model that natively emits interleaved text-image outputs, our two-stage training first builds robust thinking and reflection, then efficiently tunes the IRG pipeline in the full thinking-image trajectory data. Extensive experiments show SoTA performance, yielding absolute gains of 5-10 points on GenEval, WISE, TIIF, GenAI-Bench, and OneIG-EN, alongside substantial improvements in visual quality and fine-grained fidelity. The code, model weights and datasets will be released in: https://github.com/Osilly/Interleaving-Reasoning-Generation .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Interleaving Reasoning Generation" (IRG), a novel framework for text-to-image (T2I) generation. IRG alternates between text-based reasoning and image synthesis.  The model first generates text reasoning to guide the initial image generation.  Then, it reflects on the generated image, refining fine-grained details and visual quality while preserving semantics. To train IRG, the authors propose "Interleaving Reasoning Generation Learning" (IRGL) and create a dataset called IRGL-300K.  Experiments show IRG achieves state-of-the-art performance on multiple T2I benchmarks, demonstrating improvements in visual quality and fine-grained fidelity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of interleaving reasoning and generation isn't entirely new.  Previous works explored text-based reasoning to improve image generation.  However, IRG introduces a more sophisticated, iterative refinement process. The novelty lies in the systematic approach to both generating the initial reasoning, *and then reflecting on an *image* to improve subsequent generation*.  The two-stage training approach (initial thinking/generation and then refinement) also adds novelty.

* **Significance:** The paper shows compelling improvements on standard benchmarks, suggesting that IRG effectively enhances T2I generation. The detailed analysis of visual quality and fine-grained fidelity is a valuable contribution. Creating and open-sourcing the IRGL-300K dataset is also significant, as it provides a resource for future research. The ablation studies show the importance of different components of IRGL.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper effectively identifies the limitations of current T2I models regarding instruction following and detail preservation.
    * **Well-Defined Framework:** IRG and IRGL are clearly explained, with a logical flow.
    * **Strong Experimental Results:** The results on various benchmarks convincingly demonstrate IRG's superiority.
    * **Dataset Contribution:** The release of IRGL-300K facilitates further research in this direction.
    * **Ablation Study:** This provides insight into the design choices.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of iterative reasoning and reflection is new, each component builds on prior research.
    * **Reliance on Specific Models:** The framework's effectiveness relies on a strong base model (BAGEL).  It would be interesting to see how IRG performs with other foundation models.
    * **Computational Cost:** The paper doesn't extensively discuss the computational cost associated with the iterative refinement process.  Increased computational expense needs to be justified by a significant gain in performance.
    * **Qualitative examples are not particularly compelling:** The "obvious flaws" that red boxes point to in the initial generated image aren't particularly obvious in the visual comparison, slightly undermining the strength of the claims.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper's findings could influence future research in T2I generation by encouraging the development of more sophisticated reasoning and refinement techniques. The IRGL-300K dataset is likely to become a valuable resource for the community.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper presents a strong contribution to the T2I field with compelling results, the core ideas build upon existing methods. There's incremental improvement, particularly in the systematic interleaving and image-based reflection but not a completely groundbreaking paradigm shift. The significance of improved visual quality and fine-grained control should not be understated.

### Beyond Two-Stage Training: Cooperative SFT and RL for LLM Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06948v1)
- **Authors**: Liang Chen, Xueting Han, Li Shen, Jing Bai, Kam-Fai Wong
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has proven effective in incentivizing the reasoning abilities of large language models (LLMs), but suffers from severe efficiency challenges due to its trial-and-error nature. While the common practice employs supervised fine-tuning (SFT) as a warm-up stage for RL, this decoupled two-stage approach limits interaction between SFT and RL, thereby constraining overall effectiveness. This study introduces a novel method for learning reasoning models that employs bilevel optimization to facilitate better cooperation between these training paradigms. By conditioning the SFT objective on the optimal RL policy, our approach enables SFT to meta-learn how to guide RL's optimization process. During training, the lower level performs RL updates while simultaneously receiving SFT supervision, and the upper level explicitly maximizes the cooperative gain-the performance advantage of joint SFT-RL training over RL alone. Empirical evaluations on five reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that our method consistently outperforms baselines and achieves a better balance between effectiveness and efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces BRIDGE, a novel training framework for large language models (LLMs) that aims to improve reasoning abilities by tightly integrating supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL). The core idea is to use bilevel optimization where SFT acts as the upper-level problem, guiding RL's optimization at the lower level. This allows SFT to provide targeted guidance based on RL's performance, while RL benefits from SFT's expert knowledge. The method employs an augmented model architecture with a base model and a LoRA module, enabling cooperative adaptation between objectives. The paper demonstrates through experiments on math reasoning benchmarks that BRIDGE consistently outperforms baselines like SFT, RL-zero, cold-start (two-stage SFT+RL), and a naive alternating approach, achieving better accuracy and training efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to integrating SFT and RL for LLM reasoning by formulating it as a bilevel optimization problem. This is a departure from the common decoupled two-stage approach or simple alternating updates. The idea of SFT actively guiding RL optimization based on RL's performance is innovative. The use of a LoRA module to separate the base model and auxiliary parameters is clever, allowing for more targeted SFT guidance.

* **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing the limitations of the common two-stage training approach (cold-start) and offering a more efficient and effective way to leverage the strengths of both SFT and RL. The results demonstrate significant improvements in accuracy and training time compared to baselines, indicating a practical contribution. The method's generalizability across different LLM architectures further strengthens its impact.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the shortcomings of existing SFT+RL approaches and motivates the need for tighter integration.
    * **Novel Method:** The bilevel optimization formulation and the BRIDGE framework are well-defined and technically sound.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The experiments are comprehensive, using multiple LLMs, diverse math reasoning benchmarks, and comparisons against strong baselines. The results consistently demonstrate the superiority of BRIDGE.
    * **Training Dynamics Analysis:** The analysis of training dynamics provides valuable insights into how BRIDGE overcomes limitations of other methods like catastrophic forgetting and inefficient exploration.
    * **Cost-Benefit Analysis:** The cost-benefit analysis highlights the practical advantages of BRIDGE in terms of training time and memory usage.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:**  The evaluation focuses primarily on mathematical reasoning tasks. While strong, it would be beneficial to see results on other reasoning domains (e.g., commonsense reasoning, coding).
    * **Sensitivity to Hyperparameters:** The performance of bilevel optimization methods can be sensitive to hyperparameters like the penalty weight λ. The paper mentions an annealing schedule, but further details on tuning and robustness would be valuable.
    * **LoRA integration complexity:** The LoRA module selection can further complicate the training, as the type of Low-Rank adaptation method can impact performance.

* **Potential Influence:**  The BRIDGE framework has the potential to influence the way LLMs are trained for reasoning tasks. It provides a principled approach for integrating SFT and RL, which could lead to more efficient and performant models. The idea of using bilevel optimization to enable cooperative learning between different training paradigms could be applied to other LLM tasks as well.

* **Rigorous Rationale for Score:** While the paper has some minor weaknesses, the novelty, significance, and strong empirical results justify a high score. The bilevel optimization approach is a significant contribution to the field of LLM training, offering a more effective and efficient way to combine SFT and RL. The consistent improvements over strong baselines across different LLMs and benchmarks demonstrate the practical value of the method. The analysis of training dynamics further strengthens the paper by providing insights into why BRIDGE works.

### Revolutionizing Reinforcement Learning Framework for Diffusion Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06949v1)
- **Authors**: Yinjie Wang, Ling Yang, Bowen Li, Ye Tian, Ke Shen, Mengdi Wang
- **Abstract**: We propose TraceRL, a trajectory-aware reinforcement learning framework for diffusion language models (DLMs) that incorporates preferred inference trajectory into post-training, and is applicable across different architectures. Equipped with a diffusion-based value model that enhances training stability, we demonstrate improved reasoning performance on complex math and coding tasks. Besides, it can also be applied to adapt block-specific models to larger blocks, which improves sampling flexibility. Employing TraceRL, we derive a series of state-of-the-art diffusion language models, namely TraDo. Although smaller than 7B-scale AR models, TraDo-4B-Instruct still consistently outperforms them across complex math reasoning tasks. TraDo-8B-Instruct achieves relative accuracy improvements of 6.1% over Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and 51.3% over Llama3.1-8B-Instruct on mathematical reasoning benchmarks. Through curriculum learning, we also derive the first long-CoT DLM, outperforming Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct on MATH500 with an 18.1% relative accuracy gain. To facilitate reproducible research and practical applications, we release a comprehensive open-source framework for building, training, and deploying diffusion LLMs across diverse architectures. The framework integrates accelerated KV-cache techniques and inference engines for both inference and reinforcement learning, and includes implementations of various supervised fine-tuning and RL methods for mathematics, coding, and general tasks. Code and Models: https://github.com/Gen-Verse/dLLM-RL
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper based on the provided OCR output.

**Summary**

The paper introduces TraceRL, a trajectory-aware reinforcement learning framework designed for diffusion language models (DLMs). It addresses the mismatch between the training objective of DLMs, which typically use random masking, and their inference behavior, which is often left-to-right or block-wise. TraceRL incorporates the preferred inference trajectory into post-training, making it applicable to various DLM architectures.  The framework uses a diffusion-based value model to enhance training stability. The authors create a series of models, TraDo, using TraceRL, demonstrating improved reasoning performance on math and coding tasks compared to autoregressive models and other DLMs, even at smaller scales (4B and 8B parameters). They also introduce the first long-CoT (Chain-of-Thought) DLM. The paper also highlights the release of an open-source framework for building, training, and deploying diffusion LLMs, including features like accelerated KV-cache techniques and implementations of supervised fine-tuning and RL methods.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in the integration of trajectory awareness into the reinforcement learning process for diffusion language models.  Existing RL methods for DLMs often treat the sequence generation process as a black box, focusing solely on the final output. By explicitly considering the intermediate steps (traces) of the generation process and aligning the training objective with the actual inference path (e.g., block-wise generation), TraceRL offers a more fine-grained and potentially more effective approach to RL fine-tuning.  The diffusion-based value model is also a novel component contributing to training stability in RL for DLMs. The first Long-CoT Diffusion language model is also a significant and novel contribution.

**Significance:**

The significance of the paper stems from its potential to address a key challenge in the development and application of DLMs: improving their reasoning capabilities. The results demonstrate tangible improvements on complex tasks such as math problem-solving and code generation. The state-of-the-art performance achieved by the TraDo models, especially the 4B and 8B versions outperforming larger AR models, shows the practical value of the proposed framework. The open-source framework has a positive impact on the community as well, lowering the barrier to entry for future research. Furthermore, the success in creating a long-CoT DLM signifies that diffusion models, which are often preferred for their efficiency in generation, can compete with autoregressive models in complex reasoning tasks where chain-of-thought reasoning is critical.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the issue of mismatch between training and inference in DLMs.
*   **Well-Defined Method:** TraceRL is presented in a well-structured and understandable manner. The components (trajectory awareness, value model, shrinkage parameter, sliced training) are clearly explained.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental evaluation is thorough and comprehensive, covering a range of tasks and datasets. The reported performance gains are significant and well-supported by the data.
*   **Open-Source Framework:** The release of the open-source framework is a valuable contribution to the research community.
*   **Addressing an important and growing area of research:** Diffusion language models are increasingly important and this paper addresses a major deficiency in how to train them.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Complexity:** TraceRL introduces several components, which adds to its complexity. A more detailed analysis of the individual contribution of each component could strengthen the paper.
*   **Computational Cost:** Although the shrinkage parameter is used to reduce computation, the overall RL fine-tuning process can still be computationally demanding. A discussion of the computational resources needed to implement TraceRL would be helpful.
*   **Generalizability:** While the results are impressive on the tested benchmarks, the generalizability of TraceRL to other types of tasks and DLM architectures could be further explored. Some additional experiments on general tasks could strengthen the overall impact.
*   **Limited Comparison with Post-Training Alternative:**  The paper does compare itself against RL methods, but it does not compare as much against alternative post-training methods for Diffusion language models. A comparison to for example, SLiC, could strengthen the paper.

**Impact and Influence:**

The paper has a high potential to influence the field of DLMs. The introduced trajectory-aware RL approach and the diffusion-based value model could become standard techniques for fine-tuning DLMs for improved reasoning performance. The open-source framework will enable other researchers to build upon and extend the work presented in the paper.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of diffusion language models. The TraceRL framework addresses a critical issue in DLM training and demonstrates compelling empirical results.  While there are some weaknesses in terms of complexity, computational cost discussion, and generalizability, the overall quality of the work, the strength of the results, and the release of the open-source framework justify a high score. It will likely have a significant impact on future research in DLMs. It is not perfect because it is still an early exploration and is complex, so it misses some of the very highest accolades, but its combination of impact, significance, and novelty justifies a score of 8.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Scaling Transformer-Based Novel View Synthesis Models with Token Disentanglement and Synthetic Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06950v1)
- **Authors**: Nithin Gopalakrishnan Nair, Srinivas Kaza, Xuan Luo, Vishal M. Patel, Stephen Lombardi, Jungyeon Park
- **Abstract**: Large transformer-based models have made significant progress in generalizable novel view synthesis (NVS) from sparse input views, generating novel viewpoints without the need for test-time optimization. However, these models are constrained by the limited diversity of publicly available scene datasets, making most real-world (in-the-wild) scenes out-of-distribution. To overcome this, we incorporate synthetic training data generated from diffusion models, which improves generalization across unseen domains. While synthetic data offers scalability, we identify artifacts introduced during data generation as a key bottleneck affecting reconstruction quality. To address this, we propose a token disentanglement process within the transformer architecture, enhancing feature separation and ensuring more effective learning. This refinement not only improves reconstruction quality over standard transformers but also enables scalable training with synthetic data. As a result, our method outperforms existing models on both in-dataset and cross-dataset evaluations, achieving state-of-the-art results across multiple benchmarks while significantly reducing computational costs. Project page: https://scaling3dnvs.github.io/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of scaling transformer-based novel view synthesis (NVS) models, which are often limited by the scarcity and lack of diversity in real-world scene datasets. The authors propose to use synthetic training data generated from diffusion models to improve generalization across unseen domains. They identify that artifacts in synthetic data hinder reconstruction quality. To mitigate this, they introduce a Token-Disentangled (Tok-D) transformer block that enhances feature separation between source and target views during the transformer processing, which improves learning from synthetic data. By training with synthetic data and using the Tok-D transformer, the method achieves state-of-the-art results on multiple NVS benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The idea of using synthetic data to augment NVS training is not entirely new, but the paper makes two key contributions: 1) identifying and explicitly addressing the artifacts introduced by diffusion models in the synthetic data generation pipeline as a critical bottleneck and 2) the Tok-D transformer block for improved learning of diverse scene content, which helps to better generalize. While prior works have explored synthetic data augmentation in NVS, this paper’s specific combination of identifying synthetic data artifacts and token disentanglement is novel.

* **Significance:**  The paper addresses a significant problem in the NVS field: the limited availability of diverse real-world training data. Successfully leveraging synthetic data is crucial for scaling NVS models to handle in-the-wild scenarios. The Tok-D transformer offers a way to efficiently handle the inherent noise/artifacts introduced by synthetic data. Achieving state-of-the-art results on multiple benchmarks suggests a potentially valuable contribution to the field.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong experimental results:** The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on multiple datasets.
    * **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the limitations of current NVS models and the challenges of using synthetic data.
    * **Well-designed solution:** The Tok-D transformer block is a plausible approach to address the feature misalignment problem.
    * **Good ablation studies:** The ablation studies validate the effectiveness of the different components.
    * **Addresses an important problem:** Scaling NVS using synthetic data is crucial for real-world applications.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited analysis of failure cases:**  While the paper mentions limitations such as difficulties with dynamic scene changes, the analysis remains qualitative, and could be more insightful with a deeper quantitative study. More analysis and potentially addressing limitations in a future paper would improve the contribution.
    * **Incremental contribution:**  While the overall approach is significant, individual contributions, such as architecture choices, have connections to prior work (e.g., DiT). Although the combination is new, the parts themselves build heavily on existing literature.

* **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence future research in NVS by demonstrating a viable approach to scaling models using synthetic data. The Tok-D transformer block could inspire other researchers to explore similar architectural modifications for handling noisy or artifact-prone data sources. The work encourages further investigation into generative data, particularly how to generate/leverage synthetic data for NVS systems robust to real world scenarios.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**  The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of NVS.  The combination of using a synthetic data pipeline designed to reduce artifacts and the Tok-D transformer, leading to state-of-the-art results, is a valuable step.  The strengths outweigh the weaknesses, which include a limited exploration of failure cases and the incremental rather than revolutionary nature of the individual components. However, the positive impact on in and cross-domain generalization justifies the high score.

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 7/10

### On the Same Wavelength? Evaluating Pragmatic Reasoning in Language Models across Broad Concepts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06952v1)
- **Authors**: Linlu Qiu, Cedegao E. Zhang, Joshua B. Tenenbaum, Yoon Kim, Roger P. Levy
- **Abstract**: Language use is shaped by pragmatics -- i.e., reasoning about communicative goals and norms in context. As language models (LMs) are increasingly used as conversational agents, it becomes ever more important to understand their pragmatic reasoning abilities. We propose an evaluation framework derived from Wavelength, a popular communication game where a speaker and a listener communicate about a broad range of concepts in a granular manner. We study a range of LMs on both language comprehension and language production using direct and Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting, and further explore a Rational Speech Act (RSA) approach to incorporating Bayesian pragmatic reasoning into LM inference. We find that state-of-the-art LMs, but not smaller ones, achieve strong performance on language comprehension, obtaining similar-to-human accuracy and exhibiting high correlations with human judgments even without CoT prompting or RSA. On language production, CoT can outperform direct prompting, and using RSA provides significant improvements over both approaches. Our study helps identify the strengths and limitations in LMs' pragmatic reasoning abilities and demonstrates the potential for improving them with RSA, opening up future avenues for understanding conceptual representation, language understanding, and social reasoning in LMs and humans.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel evaluation framework, WavelengthEval, to assess the pragmatic reasoning abilities of Language Models (LMs). Inspired by the popular communication game "Wavelength," this framework involves both language comprehension and production tasks. In the production task, LMs generate clues given a pair of opposite concepts and a target value. In the comprehension task, they guess the target value based on the concepts and the provided clue. The authors evaluate a range of LMs using direct prompting, Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting, and a Rational Speech Act (RSA) approach to incorporate Bayesian pragmatic reasoning into LM inference. The study finds that larger, state-of-the-art LMs perform well on language comprehension, achieving near-human accuracy and high correlations with human judgments. CoT improves performance on language production, and RSA significantly improves performance over both direct and CoT prompting. The study also investigates the nature of conceptual representation in humans and LMs by analyzing their judgment distributions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a new dataset (WavelengthEval) and a specific methodology for evaluating pragmatic reasoning in LMs, which is a definite contribution. While the RSA framework itself isn't new, its application within this context, specifically with the inclusion of automatically generated alternatives, represents a novel implementation.
*   **Significance:** The work addresses an important and timely issue: the pragmatic reasoning capabilities of LMs, particularly as they are increasingly used as conversational agents. The findings provide valuable insights into the strengths and limitations of LMs in understanding and generating language in context. Demonstrating that RSA enhances LM performance on production is significant and suggests a pathway for improving their pragmatic abilities. However, the observation that the listener benefits more from better distribution approximation and that this, rather than better alternatives, is the limiting factor is significant.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The WavelengthEval dataset is well-motivated and captures key aspects of pragmatic communication, including shared goals, graded meanings, and world knowledge application.
    *   The use of RSA to augment LMs is a valuable exploration.
    *   The study includes a comprehensive evaluation of a range of LMs, providing a broad perspective on the current state of the art.
    *   The analysis of human and LM judgment distributions offers insights into their conceptual representations.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The study acknowledges limitations regarding the size of the dataset. Although typical for human studies, a larger dataset would provide a more robust benchmark and allow for more precise evaluation.
    *   The evaluation setup is a simplification of the full Wavelength game, focusing on single-turn interactions. Real-world interactions often involve multiple turns and more complex dynamics.
    *   While the paper explores RSA, other aspects of cost considerations in utterances and how they affect pragmatic performance are not explored.
*   **Justification:**
    The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by proposing and validating a new framework for evaluating pragmatic reasoning in LMs. The experimental results and analysis are valuable, especially the investigation of why RSA has a differential impact in production vs. comprehension. The work highlights the potential for combining RSA with LMs to improve their pragmatic abilities. However, the limitations regarding dataset size and simplified experimental setup, and that RSA, per se, has a limited impact on the LMs ability to improve comprehension do temper the excitement. Overall, the paper is an incremental advance over the current SOTA.

### H$_{2}$OT: Hierarchical Hourglass Tokenizer for Efficient Video Pose Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06956v1)
- **Authors**: Wenhao Li, Mengyuan Liu, Hong Liu, Pichao Wang, Shijian Lu, Nicu Sebe
- **Abstract**: Transformers have been successfully applied in the field of video-based 3D human pose estimation. However, the high computational costs of these video pose transformers (VPTs) make them impractical on resource-constrained devices. In this paper, we present a hierarchical plug-and-play pruning-and-recovering framework, called Hierarchical Hourglass Tokenizer (H$_{2}$OT), for efficient transformer-based 3D human pose estimation from videos. H$_{2}$OT begins with progressively pruning pose tokens of redundant frames and ends with recovering full-length sequences, resulting in a few pose tokens in the intermediate transformer blocks and thus improving the model efficiency. It works with two key modules, namely, a Token Pruning Module (TPM) and a Token Recovering Module (TRM). TPM dynamically selects a few representative tokens to eliminate the redundancy of video frames, while TRM restores the detailed spatio-temporal information based on the selected tokens, thereby expanding the network output to the original full-length temporal resolution for fast inference. Our method is general-purpose: it can be easily incorporated into common VPT models on both seq2seq and seq2frame pipelines while effectively accommodating different token pruning and recovery strategies. In addition, our H$_{2}$OT reveals that maintaining the full pose sequence is unnecessary, and a few pose tokens of representative frames can achieve both high efficiency and estimation accuracy. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmark datasets demonstrate both the effectiveness and efficiency of the proposed method. Code and models are available at https://github.com/NationalGAILab/HoT.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Hierarchical Hourglass Tokenizer (H2OT), a plug-and-play framework designed to improve the efficiency of transformer-based 3D human pose estimation from videos (VPTs). H2OT operates by progressively pruning redundant pose tokens (frames) in the intermediate layers of the transformer and then recovering the full-length sequence at the output. This strategy reduces computational cost without significantly sacrificing accuracy. The approach involves a Token Pruning Module (TPM) to select representative tokens and a Token Recovering Module (TRM) to restore the full sequence. The paper shows that a complete pose sequence is not crucial, and using select pose tokens from representative frames leads to high accuracy and efficiency. Experiments on several benchmark datasets demonstrate the framework's effectiveness and compatibility with various existing VPT architectures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper extends upon previous work (specifically, the authors' CVPR 2024 paper) by introducing a hierarchical pruning design, resulting in a more efficient and accurate pruning strategy, which is novel. Compared to other token pruning studies, the focus on video redundancy and the incorporation of token recovery strategies specific to the 3D HPE domain is also novel. The use of a parameter-free sampling pruning and interpolation recovering strategies significantly improves the robustness and inference time.
*   **Significance:** Improving the efficiency of VPTs is significant because it makes them more practical for real-world applications, particularly on resource-constrained devices. The H2OT framework offers a general and flexible way to reduce the computational burden of these models without substantially decreasing their performance. The framework’s plug-and-play nature is a strong point. The findings about the redundancy of full pose sequences are valuable to the community and could inform future research. The empirical results consistently show improvements in efficiency and competitive accuracy across different VPT architectures, strengthening the paper's significance.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper presents a clear and well-motivated problem.
    *   The H2OT framework is well-designed and explained.
    *   Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach across multiple datasets and architectures.
    *   The parameter-free strategy using the sampling pruning and interpolation recovering greatly improves the robustness and inference time.
    *   The ablation studies are comprehensive and informative, providing insights into the design choices and hyperparameters.
    *   Comparison with the state-of-the-art approaches clearly demonstrates the superior performance of H2OT
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the H2OT's performance compared to that of other token pruners and the other baselines shows a relative improvement, it is important to acknowledge that the improvement relative to other highly developed methods may sometimes be marginal.
    *   The gains are heavily benchmarked on a single, albeit popular, dataset. Performance and generalization to more varied pose datasets could improve the contribution.
    *   The paper could be strengthened by additional discussion of the limitations of H2OT, such as scenarios where the assumption of temporal redundancy breaks down (e.g., very rapid or unpredictable motions).
    *   More visual examples that clearly illustrate the impact of token pruning and recovery would enhance the paper. The current examples in figure 10 do not easily allow readers to understand what information loss, if any, occurs.

*   **Potential Influence:** H2OT has the potential to influence future research in several ways:
    *   It can serve as a baseline for evaluating new methods for efficient VPTs.
    *   It can inspire the development of new token pruning and recovery strategies.
    *   It can encourage further exploration of the trade-off between computational cost and accuracy in pose estimation.
    *   The strategy can be adapted to other transformer-based video processing tasks beyond 3D human pose estimation.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper represents a strong, incremental advance in the field of 3D human pose estimation. The proposed framework is well-designed, empirically validated, and addresses a critical challenge (computational efficiency) in a practical and general way. While the performance improvements are not always groundbreaking and some limitations exist, the contributions are significant enough to make a noteworthy impact on the community. Therefore, a score of 7 is appropriate.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
